| Review fixes of the integrated observability branches on `integrate-observability` (2026-10-19) | The adversarial review of the integration found six things; each was checked in the code and fixed with its failing test first. **(1) The capacity cron during a maintenance cycle (medium).** `startNodeMaintenance` closes placement on an online node, so the headroom check failed and `runNodeCapacity` reserved a node addition (an adoption, or an order once an hour, which a standing approval lets through). It now answers `maintenance` and buys nothing while a node of the region that is not lost holds an open maintenance order whose `placement_closed_at` equals the node's `database_placement_closed_at`. The offline half (the reboot) answers `maintenance` too, ahead of `observations_stale`. A closure an operator set, and the closure a failed cycle leaves, still buy as before. Test: `node-maintenance.test.ts`, which also covers the cron buying again once the cycle is done and for an operator's closure. **(2) Route labels and alert texts.** `DIAGNOSTIC_ROUTES` lacked the three diagnostics routes, so their failures were logged as `unmatched` like an attacker path; the same gap held for eight older routes (`/v1/nodes/:id`, `/v1/nodes/:id/database-placement`, `/v1/regions/:id`, `/v1/fleet/releases`, both `/v1/infrastructure-backups` routes, `/node/v1/archive-credentials/:id` and `/node/v1/openapi.json`); all are labelled, and a test compares every route the app registers with the set. The `node_unexpected_reboot` texts (the alert action, the `InfraAlertKind` description, the operational-health route and `infrastructure-backups.md`) and the reboot line of the probe runbook now send the operator to the list for the report with `expected_reboot` false and then to `GET /v1/nodes/{id}/diagnostics/{diagnostic_id}` for the journal tail, which the list does not hold. **(3) The boot report.** `previous_boot_id` is the boot of the lines (the `_BOOT_ID` of the journal, which journald prints as 32 hex digits), no longer the stored boot: with B1 stored, B2's report refused and the agent restarting at B3, the report now says B2. The stored boot is the fallback where the journal names none, and an agent that stored none still claims none. A report that Cloudflare could not take (network, 408, 429, 5xx) is sent again within the boot, waiting 2 s doubling to 60 s (`report_retry`, `report_retry_maximum`); any other refusal ends the attempt for this start, as before. The API keeps one row per `boot_id` and answers a repeat with the stored one, so the repeat is safe. **The API cannot tell a missed report, and no machinery was added for it:** a report whose `previous_boot_id` has no row of its own is also what the first reboot of a node looks like, because the first start posts nothing. An operator reads a gap from the list: the `previous_boot_id` of a row that is not the `boot_id` of an older row. **(4) `os_previous_observed_at`.** The hello that changes the boot now stores the newer of D1's `last_observed_at` and the newest sample the NodeActor holds (D1 holds the first sample of the running minute until the alarm, up to 65 s older), so a fast cycle is no longer judged unexpected and a crash soon after a cycle without a reboot is no longer expected. Test: `node-actor.test.ts` (two samples in one minute, a new-boot hello before the alarm, then a hello of the same boot). **(5) `os_converged` after a release that drops a package.** The cycle now purges after it installs: the installed packages the lock does not name, with `dpkg --purge` (dpkg refuses a package an installed one depends on, and no cascade removes a locked package), after `dpkg-query` marked the essential ones, which are left alone. Never a kernel image (Debian keeps the previous one for the boot menu and the digest leaves idle ones out), never a name that fails the pattern of `infra/node-image/lock.ts`, and nothing for a lock that names no kernel image, so an empty or truncated lock cannot empty the node. `pgcf-node-base` does not exist; the image has no first-party package, and `compareInstalled` fails the image build unless its installed set equals the lock, so a node from the image holds exactly the lock. A refused purge fails the cycle with `packages_not_installed`. Tests: `update.rs` (the set, the guards, the essential filter) and `service.rs` (lock B drops a package of lock A; after the cycle the digest equals the digest of B's packages, the idle kernel stays, no reboot). `Node::purge_packages` has only run against the Local double, never `dpkg` itself. **Checks run on the tip before this note:** `pnpm typecheck` and `tsc` of `scripts/ci` and `scripts/operations`, eslint, prettier, plan privacy, `cargo fmt`, `cargo clippy -D warnings` (native and wasm), Mermaider update and check (the manifest fingerprint changed with the sources), contracts 18 files and 214 tests (the native generators pass `--check`), API 102 files and 935 tests, `cargo test --workspace --locked` with `TMPDIR=/tmp/na` (556 passed and 6 ignored; node agent 419), edge 119 and its 2 node tests, cli 31, e2e 66 (2 skipped, none failed) and the script tests 63. The `apps/edge-rust` node test needs the Worker build and was not run here; CI builds it. One earlier run of the whole cargo workspace failed `a_start_that_outlasts_its_wake_and_then_fails_leaves_the_database_wakeable` (a 300 ms start deadline) while Prettier and Mermaider ran beside it; alone it passed three times, and the whole workspace passed on the rerun with nothing else running. CI (workflow dispatch on the branch, run 39198959320, commit `9419c195`, the tip before this note): green (check and node-image). Nothing is deployed or accepted live |
| Repair of the second review of the integrated observability branches on `integrate-observability` (2026-10-19) | A second review of the review fixes raised three findings; each was checked before it was fixed, and the code ones were fixed with their failing test first. **(1) A purge that dpkg refuses after a kernel install (confirmed).** `install_packages` installed, re-read the packages and purged before it rebooted. A refused purge therefore failed the order with `packages_not_installed` before the reboot, and the resubmitted cycle planned no reboot (the kernel was installed already), purged, and answered `done` on the old kernel; only `os_converged` false showed it. The reboot now follows the install at once and the cycle returns; the cycle that the agent runs for the order that is still open after the boot finds nothing to install, purges, checks and answers, so a refused purge fails the order on the new kernel and the resubmitted cycle purges and answers `done`. The purge also leaves out the packages whose `${Protected}` is yes. In a Debian trixie container without network (dpkg 1.22.22), `dpkg-query -W -f '${Package}\t${Essential}\t${Protected}\n' login libgcc-s1 bash` printed `login\tno\tyes`, `libgcc-s1\tno\tyes` and `bash\tyes\tno`, and `dpkg --purge login` and `dpkg --purge libgcc-s1` ended with "this is a protected package; it should not be removed"; the filter read `${Essential}` only. Tests: `update.rs` (`without_protected`; red first against the old filter, which kept `bash` and `login` in the list), `service.rs` (`a_kernel_reboots_the_node_before_a_purge_that_dpkg_may_refuse`: dpkg refuses the purge once; red first by timing out for the reboot; then the order fails with `packages_not_installed` on the new kernel and a second order is `done`, the package gone). `Node::purge_packages` has still only run against the Local double; the new query format is the one the container printed. **(2) A boot report that reaches the API before its boot's hello (confirmed).** The agent does not order the two (the link connects in the background, and the report goes after the first pull), so the API judged the report against `nodes.last_observed_at`, which lags the newest sample by up to a minute: with a sample flushed, an order made after it and a newer sample held only by the actor, the verdict was `expected_reboot` false and `node_unexpected_reboot` stood for 24 h. A boot report that claims an earlier boot and arrives while the node's `os_boot_id` is not its `boot_id` is now refused with 503 `capacity_exhausted`, reason `not_ready`, and `Retry-After: 5`; nothing is stored, and the agent's retry (2 s doubling to 60 s) sends it again after the hello, which stores the sample the actor holds as `os_previous_observed_at`. `rebootWasAsked` reads only that column. A report of an agent that knew no earlier boot claims nothing and is not held back. The OpenAPI text and status table of `POST /node/v1/diagnostics` name the 503. Test: `node-diagnostics.test.ts` drives the previous boot's hello, a sample, the order and a second sample (held by the actor) over the link, sends the report before the new hello (503, nothing stored, red first: it was accepted), then after the hello (`expected_reboot` true); the other tests of the file say the hello through the `reboot` helper, as the agent does. **(3) Texts (confirmed).** Plan 9.3 names the purge and the reboot before it, plan 9.9 the retry within the boot and the 503, and the SEC-03 acceptance row the purge after the boot; `infra/node-image/README.md` names protected packages; `docs/operations/recovery.md` says that until a cycle ends the capacity cron buys no node in the region at all, because the guard in `runNodeCapacity` never compares the shortage with the node under maintenance (a waiting database that no node could take, even with that node open, waits too). **Checks run on the tip before this note:** `cargo fmt`, `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings` and clippy of `pgcf-edge-rust` for wasm32, `cargo test -p pgcf-node-agent --locked` with a private `TMPDIR` and `--test-threads=6` (420 passed, 6 ignored), the other workspace crates (all passed in the `--no-fail-fast` run), the seven `packages/contracts/native/generate*.ts --check`, API 102 files and 936 tests, `pnpm lint`, `pnpm typecheck`, `tsc` of `scripts/ci` and `scripts/operations`, Prettier, plan privacy (0 findings), the script tests (63) and Mermaider update and check. Three full workspace runs on this laptop failed 2, 3 and 17 node-agent tests (`build_failed` while a database was built, `ArchiveTimeout`) while other sessions held 10 to 17 of the 32 System V shared-memory segments that macOS allows (`kern.sysv.shmmni`) for their own PostgreSQL servers; the failing tests differed from run to run and passed alone, and the run with six test threads passed whole. Not run here: the `apps/edge-rust` node test (needs the Worker build; CI builds it). CI (workflow dispatch on the branch, commit `24dc7ea2`, the tip before this note): the first run (39204157786) failed `agent::power::tests::a_database_sleeps_with_a_proof_and_wakes_through_admission_against_its_record` (the test gateway's `received()` had not yet seen the last message the agent sent; the test and `power.rs` are not touched by this change), and the rerun (39204855109) was green (check and node-image). Nothing is deployed or accepted live |
| First live maintenance cycle on EU1 (2026-10-19) | Main `5cb2a4e4`, release `sha-5cb2a4e4a97f` (kernel 6.12.113-1 from the refreshed lock, expat 2.9.0, libpq5 18.6, the hardened image files, node OS identity, previous-boot journal upload, read-only diagnostics, the maintenance route). Deploy order: the API with migrations 0072–0075 first (0072 deleted the registered releases), then the registration and the region assignment (revision 0 to 1) at 07:49:28 UTC; the old agent's hello without `os` was refused in between, so the node was without samples for about 7 minutes (the 275 MB registration took most of it). The agent restarted and reported the new release 12 s after the assignment and uploaded its boot report. `POST /v1/nodes/{id}/maintenance` at 07:53:54: placement closed, wakes held, the packages installed from the lock, the node rebooted, the check ran, the order was `done` at 07:55:02, **67 s in all**; the node reported kernel 6.12.113+deb13-amd64 and `os_converged` true 56 s after the call (the shutdown line of the journal is 07:54:27.6, the agent's start 17 s into the new boot) and placement reopened by 07:55:08. The reboot was recognised as asked for (`expected_reboot` true) and its report carried the previous boot's journal tail, 467 entries (pgcf-gateway 147, pgcf-agent 57, pgcf-cloudflared 31, kernel 16, init 212), which is the evidence the VM stall of 2026-10-18 lacked. Afterwards SQL through Cloudflare worked on a cold database (the ledger of the power-cut test intact: 3,040 rows) and the gateway hostname answered 401 without the token. The purge of packages a lock drops (`dpkg --purge`) was not exercised: this lock dropped none. Not yet run: the cold-wake comparison after the reboot, and the hardened image files (they reach a node only through a reinstall or the base package, SEC-07) |
| Wake attribution on branch `wake-attribution` (2026-10-19) | After `wake-node-path` the start (97–539 ms live, 99–184 ms in Stage 1) is the largest part of the node's time and had no split; `release` was written as 0 on every wake, and `refresh_roles` and `after_ready` were in no phase. **Phases.** `WAKE_PHASES` has `roles` (the page's roles written before anyone logs in, timed on its own); `release` is measured, from the end of `start` to the writer in the gateway's table, and holds `roles`, so `admission`, `queue_wait`, `start` and `release` are the top-level parts of a wake and `roles` is part of `release`. A wake of a database that already runs has no phases (it carried `release` 0). The CPU quota (`after_ready`) is set after the writer is published and is in no phase. **`wake_detail`.** For every wake that starts the unit (not a late start, not a running database) the agent spawns a task that is never awaited on the wake path; `Timing::detail` (2 s) after the release it emits one event through the node-events ring (`crate::timing::emit_event`, so it reaches Workers Logs as `node_event`), numbers only, 9 fields of the 16 the contract allows: `job_to_fork_us` (the service's `ExecMainStartTimestampMonotonic`, read over the agent's zbus connection, minus the agent's `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` just before `StartUnit`; systemd records there when it creates the main process, which is `systemd-executor` until it executes postgres, not when postgres is executed), `fork_to_handoff_us` (to `ExecMainHandoffTimestampMonotonic`, systemd 256 and later, read over the same proxy; it is when the executor is about to execute postgres, so this is the time of the unit's sandbox that systemd sets up: the new network namespace, the mount namespace of `ProtectSystem=strict`, `PrivateTmp`, `PrivateDevices`, the `/dev/shm` tmpfs, `ProtectProc`, the seccomp filter and the switch to the database's user), `handoff_to_starting_us`, `starting_to_shut_down_us` and `shut_down_to_ready_us` (journald's `__MONOTONIC_TIMESTAMP` of the lines of this invocation, from `journalctl -o json --output-fields=MESSAGE,__MONOTONIC_TIMESTAMP _SYSTEMD_INVOCATION_ID=<id>`, matching only `starting PostgreSQL`, `database system was shut down at` and `database system is ready to accept connections`; `units/journal.rs` returns the gaps and no message text, and a start that was a crash recovery writes no `shut down at` line and gives no gaps), `nr_throttled` and `throttled_usec` (the deltas of `/sys/fs/cgroup/pgcf.slice/pgcf-pg@<id>.service/cpu.stat` between the release and the read), and `steal_ms` with `steal_window_ms` (the delta of the `steal` column of the first `/proc/stat` line, summed over the cores, from just before `StartUnit`, after the admission queue, to the read, and the milliseconds in between; one clock tick is 10 ms). A field that could not be read is left out; a unit that started again in the 2 s has no gaps. The one addition on the wake path is a read of `/proc/stat` just before `StartUnit`, after admission (`start_unit` takes it, so the start of a new or a recovered database reads it too). **Lab, not EU1.** The journal fixtures (`apps/node-agent/src/units/fixtures/`) and the `cpu.stat` text of the sample test were recorded in a Debian 13 container with systemd 257.13 as PID 1 (arm64, the laptop's Docker VM, 6 vCPUs; `--privileged` with the container's own cgroup namespace, because the host's puts the units under a path other than the `/sys/fs/cgroup/pgcf.slice` that `cpu_stat_path` reads), the repository's own `pgcf-pg@.service` and PostgreSQL 18.6: a start after a clean stop (four lines) and one after `SIGKILL` of the postmaster (nine lines, no `shut down at`). The new lab test `a_start_has_a_timeline_of_the_fork_the_handoff_and_the_journals_three_messages` runs `Units::timeline` against that systemd and journal; each gap is a subtraction that gives nothing when the request, the fork, the handoff and `starting PostgreSQL` are not in that order, so the test's `Some` is the order (7 of 7 `units::lab` tests pass there, in three runs). Five starts: job to fork 12.5 / 2.4 / 2.2 / 2.5 / 2.2 ms, fork to handoff 15.0 / 27.2 / 30.5 / 33.8 / 22.4 ms, handoff to `starting PostgreSQL` 10.2 / 11.6 / 11.3 / 11.5 / 10.7 ms, then 2.9 / 1.6 / 1.5 / 1.6 / 1.4 ms to `shut down at` and 0.04 / 1.4 / 1.3 / 1.4 / 1.3 ms to ready. Of the 25–45 ms from the fork to the postmaster's first log line, 60–75 % (15–34 ms) are the executor's sandbox and about 11 ms are PostgreSQL's way to its first line, which is why the fork is not read as the exec: counted from `ExecMainStartTimestampMonotonic` alone, systemd's sandbox would be charged to PostgreSQL. These are laptop-VM numbers; they say that the instrument works and that the sandbox is the larger part there, not what Contabo's vCPU does. The gaps are journald's receive times: two lines PostgreSQL wrote in the same millisecond were 0.2 and 5.4 ms apart in the journal in two captures, so a gap of a few ms is not resolved. **Tests.** Red on `main`: the parser over the recorded journals (gaps of the clean start, none for the crash, none when any one of the three lines is missing, lines that are no JSON or whose message is bytes skipped, a handoff after the first line gives none); the wake with a changed password records `roles` > 0 and a `release` that holds it (`roles` was not a phase; the publish of the writer is real and too fast in that test to show that the `release` timer stops after it, so the test shows the writer in the table and `release` holding `roles`, not the order of the timer and the publish); a wake that waited 2 s in the admission queue has a `steal_window_ms` below its `queue_wait` (red before the baseline moved: 2512 ms against 2387 ms); the API's `node_wake` test, which gets 400 for `roles` on the old contract. Also: the `cpu.stat` parser on the text of a unit throttled by `CPUQuota=10%` in the lab (46 of 47 periods), the deltas (counters that went back give none), the line of the event through the node-events contract, and a wake that emits exactly one `wake_detail`, not before its delay, and none for a wake of a running database. **Deploy order:** the API with the new `WAKE_PHASES` first (it refuses a `roles` phase it does not know with `invalid_request`, and the agent drops a refused batch), then the node release. CI run 39189853499 (`workflow_dispatch` on `1b0f31da`): `check` and `node-image` green. **Not measured:** everything on EU1; see Open, Stage 5 |
| DatabaseActor admission without DDL on the read path, and one `actor_admit` line per admission, on branch `actor-admit` (2026-10-19) | **Found.** `admissionState()` ran `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS database_snapshot` on every call and `storedTicket()` ran `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS database_ticket` on every call (`saveTicket` called it again), so every `admitWake`, an awake one that writes nothing included, executed DDL. `sqlite3_stmt_readonly()` returns 0 for `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` when the table exists and 1 for a `SELECT` on `sqlite_master` (checked on SQLite 3.51.0 through the C API). The workerd half is taken from the research synthesis and not re-read here: `ActorSqlite::onWrite` starts an implicit transaction and holds the output gate on the commit, so such an Actor answers only after a commit. **Changed.** Each instance keeps which of the two tables it has seen, read once with a `SELECT count(*) FROM sqlite_master WHERE type='table' AND name=?`; `admissionState()` finds no snapshot and `storedTicket()` no ticket when the table is not there, and `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` runs only in `saveAdmissionState` and `saveTicket`, once per instance. What is written and when is unchanged. **Logged.** `admitWake` writes one `actor_admit` line after its result is known: `database_id`, `waiter_id` (the Edge's connection id, so it joins `conn_admission` on `cid`), `path` (`snapshot`, `ticket`, `ensure_awake` or `refused`), `reused` (for a ticket; null otherwise), `instance_age_ms` (the time since the instance was constructed: 0 when this call built it, the time since an earlier call did otherwise, which is usually a node report's publish), `isolate_actors` (Actors constructed in the isolate so far, this one included) and `at_ms`; never the role, a password or a token. **Correction (review of `6a2ddddb`).** The first version logged `fresh_instance` and `fresh_isolate`, which marked the first `admitWake` of the instance and of the isolate; a node report publishes the snapshot before any connect and builds the Actor, so a connect to a warm instance logged both as true (the branch's own test showed it: the awake database's snapshot was published before its first `admitWake`, and the test expected `fresh_instance` true). They are gone. There is no isolate age: `Date.now()` and `performance.now()` are 0 in the global scope of workerd (measured with `wrangler dev --local` and a scratch Worker; the handler's `Date.now()` was the wall clock), so a module-evaluation timestamp cannot be taken. `isolate_actors` 1 says the isolate had no DatabaseActor before this one; the isolate may have served other classes, so it is not proof of a new isolate. **Tests.** In `admit-wake.test.ts`, red on `c46d838c` and green now: an awake admission and a reused ticket run no `CREATE`, `INSERT`, `UPDATE`, `DELETE`, `REPLACE`, `DROP` or `ALTER` through `SqlStorage.exec` (the red run listed `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS database_snapshot` three times); an Actor with a presence and no snapshot or ticket answers and creates neither table; the `actor_admit` lines carry the expected `path` and `reused` and exactly the listed fields; a second test fakes `Date` and pins `instance_age_ms` (5000 for an Actor a publish built 5 s earlier, 0 for one the admission built, 7 after 7 ms) and `isolate_actors` (one more when a new Actor is built, unchanged otherwise), and was red against a constant age and against a counter that skips. The real Actor shares the isolate with the Edge in `apps/edge/test/edge.test.ts`, so its seven line counts now count every line but the `actor_admit` ones (a filter on `conn_admission` alone made the event assertion pass whatever the line was and stopped the counts from seeing any other line). Local: the API suite (100 files, 896 tests), the Edge suite (2 node tests, 119 Vitest tests), `pnpm typecheck`, lint, format and the `scripts/ci` tests pass; the Rust Edge Worker test needs `worker-build`, which is not installed locally, and ran only in CI. CI run 39186689253 on `85683239` (workflow_dispatch on the branch): `check` and `node-image` green. After the review (the age fields, the Edge filter, `origin/main` merged in): CI run 39189545213 on `21b21ca7` (workflow_dispatch on the branch): `check` and `node-image` green. **Not measured.** The effect on `admit_ms` (11.2, Stage 5). |
| Bench arms for a batched first request, on branch `bench-arms` (2026-10-19) | **Code and tests only; no live number yet (the measurement is in 11.2).** **Checked against the repository first.** Confirmed: the Edge serves `POST /sql` (`apps/edge/src/sql.ts`, the Neon driver's `neon()` and `transaction()`); the gateway holds a request that carries a v5 wake ticket through the wake (`wake()` in `apps/native-gateway/src/sql.rs`, logged as `wake_path` `cold`); `conn_admission` and `sql_admission` both log the Edge's `colo`; `GET /healthz` is answered before any admission. Different from the brief: the repository's probe Worker had no benchmark route (the header of `saas-bench` described a privately deployed Worker), so `/saas-bench` is new there, the `ws` arm included; the figures "statements 2–5 cost 96–324 ms" and "the public hostname costs 66–135 ms more than a service binding" are in neither the plan nor the code (the plan has +0.27–0.46 s for statements 2–5, about 300 ms more for the public hostname on a whole first request, and no measurable cost on warm connects), so nothing here relies on them; the driver's README documents `transaction()` as the way to send several queries in one non-interactive transaction and does not call it the fastest. **Built.** `saas-bench run --mode ws,http-batch,http-auth-then-batch` (default `ws`, `call` takes one): a database is cold once per idle period, so each round gives each database one arm and the arms take turns by round and by database; `--rounds` must be a whole number of turns, so each database meets each arm equally often. `ws` is unchanged (five round trips on `/v2`); `http-batch` is one `sql.transaction()` of the five statements, without the BEGIN and COMMIT that statements 4 and 5 carry on the WebSocket, because the driver supplies them (one round trip: first answer equals whole request, and there is no connect time); `http-auth-then-batch` is the session lookup as `sql.query()` and one `sql.transaction()` of the other four. `statements_ms` has one entry per round trip. Over HTTP, which has no connect step, a first request that fails in a way that tells of the connection (an admission refusal `53300`, a wake not ready `57P03`, a wrong password `28P01`, class `08`, or no SQLSTATE: a network or server error) is `failed_at` `connect`, as on the WebSocket, so failure locations compare across arms; a statement's own error names the statement (the first of a transaction). The request now ends at the last answer, not one clock reading later (the existing test's 120 ms became 110 ms on the same fake clock). The last line holds `arms`: per arm the summary and the verdict `{n, over_1s, allowed_over_1s, result}` on the whole request. Misses are failed calls (a call skipped because the database was awake included) and requests not under 1 s; `allowed_over_1s` is the exact binomial rule, the most misses that a p95 of 1 s would show with at most 5 % chance (59 samples allow 0, 93 allow 1, 124 allow 2, 153 allow 3, 181 allow 4); `too_few` below 59. On the laptop the HTTP arms use `neon()` with `fetchEndpoint` at `/sql` on connections of their own per call (a call's requests share one, none borrows a socket a neighbour warmed, as a WebSocket never does). The probe Worker (`scripts/e2e/probe/worker.ts`, route `/saas-bench`) takes `mode`, records its own `colo` (`request.cf.colo`) in every answer and `ws_open_ms` (when the socket opened) for a WebSocket call, and with `mode=healthz` makes two back-to-back GETs of `/healthz` on the public hostname and on an optional `EDGE` service binding (odd calls start with the binding; `ray_colo` is read from the `cf-ray` of each answer); `saas-bench healthz --samples n --idle-s s` drives it and prints the p50 and p95 of the first and the second GET per path; a sample with a GET not answered 200 (an error page answers fast) is not ok, counts in `failures`, stays out of the percentiles and makes the exit code 1. The session code the laptop and the Worker share is `scripts/e2e/src/saas-session.ts` (it replaced the laptop's own `open`); `safeProbeError` keeps `NeonDbError`, the driver's class for an error answered over HTTP. The rig's `/sql` shim (`apps/edge/bench/rig.ts`) answers a transaction as the gateway does (`{"results":[…]}` inside BEGIN and COMMIT, a 400 with the error fields on failure), so the HTTP arms run end to end through the production Edge on a real PostgreSQL; the shim still runs in the database "postgres", not in the one a startup message names, so the test loads the dataset there too. **Rate limits.** HTTP requests count in the buckets `["sql", database, role, network]` (120 per 60 s per Cloudflare location in the example configuration), `["sql", database]` (120 per 60 s) and, from a service binding, 1,200 per 60 s. One cold sample is 1 admission for `http-batch` and 2 for `http-auth-then-batch`, once per idle period of an hour or more, plus 1 or 2 per follow-up; `/healthz` takes none. The example limits would not refuse a run, so nothing is raised; Dev's configured values are private and were not read (11.2 says to check them). **Tests first** (each seen failing before the code): the batch (same five statements and row counts, no BEGIN or COMMIT), the round trips of each arm, a batch's `Timed` (first answer equals request, no connect time), the thresholds 59/0, 93/1, 124/2, an arm's verdict with failures as misses, the rotation, `--mode` and its balanced-design check, the Worker's answer (`colo`, `ws_open_ms`), the healthz pair and its summary, and the error class. **Locally green:** E2E scripts 89 tests (66 before the branch), among them the rig test through the production Edge in workerd on PostgreSQL 18 (the three arms from the command line, and the probe Worker's own code with Node's WebSocket and fetch); typecheck, eslint, prettier, plan privacy, Mermaider update and check (the manifest fingerprint moved). CI (workflow dispatch on the branch, run 39186783796, commit `b57c208c`): green, `check` in 17 min 30 s and `node-image` in 8 min 6 s. **Review on 2026-10-19, each finding checked before it was fixed:** (1) `healthz` took the time of any answer: a run in which the public hostname answered 403, 502 or 530 printed `ok: true`, exited 0 and put the error pages' times into the p50 and p95 that step (5) of 11.2 records; a sample with a GET not answered 200 is now a failure (not ok, out of the percentiles, exit 1), with a summary test and a command-line test with a 502, both red before. (2) Step (3) of 11.2 told to read the Edge's `colo` from `conn_admission` during the `healthz` runs, which write none (`/healthz` is answered before any admission); the step is reworded. (3) The threshold for 4 misses is 181 samples, not 182 (the rule first allows 4 at 181; 180 allows 3): the code was right and the row wrong, and the test now pins 152, 153, 180 and 181. (4) Nothing guarded the laptop's own connection per call of the HTTP arms (the suite stayed green with the driver's shared `fetch`); a test with a local `/sql` server now records the connection of each request and requires one connection per call and another for each follow-up, and it goes red when the per-call agent is removed. (5) The HTTP arms named `auth` for a refusal, a wake not ready or a network error on the first request, where the WebSocket arm names `connect`; they now name `connect` for those and keep the statement for its own errors (test red before). (6) origin/main (`153ee614`) is merged, both rows of 11.1 kept; E2E scripts 89 tests, typecheck, eslint, prettier, plan privacy and Mermaider green. CI on the fixed tip (workflow dispatch on the branch, run 39189635060, commit `14b9dde3`): green, `check` in 13 min 6 s and `node-image` in 8 min 26 s. |
| Placement no longer waits 30 minutes for each new database (2026-10-19) | Found while creating the 300-database population for Stage 5.6: after eight 1 GiB databases in ten minutes every create stayed at "Waiting for verified regional capacity" although the node used 4-7 % of its RAM, because the databases placed in the last 30 minutes counted at their full class memory (8,192 MiB plus the next one against the 80 % limit of an 11,966 MiB node), a ceiling of about eight 1 GiB or thirty-two 256 MiB databases per 30 minutes per node. The pending memory now counts only the databases placed after the node's newest sample, in the read (`placementNodes`) and in the write guard (`nodeLoadGuard`, which no longer takes a time window), and the active RAM of the newest sample (`ram_now_ppm`, from `memory.available_bytes`) stands in for the 30-minute peak when it is higher. Tests: the pending-memory case, the newest-sample case and the sample reading in `placement.test.ts`; the API suite passes. The node's admission (4.6) is unchanged and keeps awake memory inside its budget. Live: the population creation resumes at once after the API deploy |
| Alerts: a lost node is announced only while a database is placed on it (2026-10-19) | The webhook delivered four active episodes at 10:57 UTC; two were `regional_node_stale` for retired lost nodes (one in each region) that held no database. `infrastructure-alerts.ts` now counts a node marked lost as failing only while a database that is not deleted is still placed on it; a stale node still counts. Tests first: `database-alerts.test.ts` (no database: silent; with a database: announced, and the episode ends when it is deleted). The texts in the API description, the contracts, the operations runbook and the handover say the same. Main `05e413c5` |
| The infrastructure backup failure is logged and its cause fixed (2026-10-19) | The daily D1 backup failed since 2026-10-17 and the Workflow recorded only "execution failed". `finishInfrastructureBackup` now writes `infrastructure_backup_failed` with the run, the code, the error class and a detail that cannot hold a URL, a key or a path (main `af9d7b34`). The cause came from the failed Workflow instances through the Cloudflare API (step `capture-encrypted-daily-control-backup-1`): the export read passed `redirect: "error"`, which the Workers runtime does not implement (`TypeError: Invalid redirect value`), so every attempt of every day failed before it read a byte; the tests injected a fetcher that never built a Request. The read now passes `redirect: "manual"` and refuses the 3xx answer; the test fetcher builds a `Request` like workerd does, and a new test shows a redirect is refused and not followed |
| EU1 carries only the deny-all Contabo firewall (2026-10-19) | `pgcf-nodes-deny-all` (one rule: drop all inbound) was assigned to EU1 through the API and the lab firewall `pgcf-stage1-lab-eu1` (operator SSH, drop) was removed. EU1's observation stayed fresh (14 s old after the change) and a SQL statement on a sleeping database through the Edge answered 2.3 s later including the Python start-up, so the Tunnel and the agent's outbound path do not depend on an inbound rule. Contabo accepts an instance in several firewalls (the first POST added a second one) |
| A create that a restart interrupted resumes (2026-10-19) | The Contabo-panel restart of EU1 at 10:51 UTC interrupted a create after its base backup was in the archive and before the state said built. The agent retried the same entry and refused it forever with `prefix_not_empty`, because `build_create` refused any backup in the archive although the build is meant to be re-entrant. The guard now lets a re-run through when every backup in the archive carries the system identifier of the cluster on this volume (the volume belongs to one storage generation and the archive path to one operation), and still refuses a backup of another cluster, a backup without an identifier, or a missing local cluster; nothing in the archive is deleted. Tests first (red with the live symptom, then green in a PostgreSQL 18 container: the resume, the foreign backup next to its own, the pure decision); the whole node-agent suite passed (451). On branch `create-resume`, reviewed by Opus: approve. It reaches EU1 with the next release |
| Cold page cache: residency in `wake_detail` and autoprewarm for every database, on branch `cold-cache` (2026-10-19) | **Found live (EU1, 2026-10-19).** A cold first request of 6–8 statements took a median of 789 ms with the databases' files partly cached and 1,085 ms (service binding) / 1,309 ms (public hostname) right after a maintenance reboot had emptied the page cache. The node path was the same (admission 1–3 ms, `start` p50 234 ms) and the statements were slower: the first (session lookup) 90–98 ms instead of 27–33, the 50-row list 68–70 instead of 45–50, an insert 44–47 instead of 18–23. 5.9 named `pg_prewarm` with `autoprewarm` for this case. **Telemetry.** `apps/node-agent/src/cache.rs` counts the cached pages of a bounded file set with `cachestat(2)` (Linux 6.5, system call 451 through `libc::syscall`; other systems return none and the fields are left out): `global/`, `pg_xact/`, the newest WAL segment and the files of the user databases below `base/` (object ID 16384 and up), newest-modified first, at most 64 files and 256 MiB of file length together, at most 16,384 directory entries looked at in a directory. The data directory is the database user's and the agent reads as root, so a link at the data directory, at `global`, `pg_xact`, `pg_wal`, `base` or a database's directory is not listed (`symlink_metadata`, as `proof::read_control` does for `data` and `global`), a link at a file is not opened (`O_NOFOLLOW` covers the last part of the path only, which is why the directories are checked first), and a pipe or a device is not opened (`O_NONBLOCK`, a regular file by `fstat`); a link put in place between the check and the listing is followed once, for the opening of at most 64 files without reading them. The first version listed through a link (a review found `pg_xact` linked to another directory giving that directory's files), and the test that was red on it is `a_link_in_place_of_a_directory_is_not_followed`. `Machine::cache_residency` carries it: the node calls `cachestat`, the test machine counts the same files with `mincore`. `try_wake` starts the reading in `spawn_blocking` once it holds the database's flow and knows it will start (before the volume checks) and never waits for it; the `wake_detail` task joins it for at most 50 ms after its 2 s and leaves the six fields out when it is late (`cache_files`, `cache_pages`, `cache_resident`, `cache_dirty`, `cache_evicted`, `cache_recently_evicted`: 15 of the 16 fields the contract allows). A reading slow enough to overlap the start counts the few pages the start has read by then (`pg_control`, one WAL segment, `pg_xact`). **Autoprewarm.** `settings::postgresql_conf` writes `shared_preload_libraries = 'pg_prewarm'`, `pg_prewarm.autoprewarm = on` and `pg_prewarm.autoprewarm_interval = 0` for every class (the dump is written at shutdown only, so a running database pays nothing); the small class has `max_worker_processes = 4` (was 2) and keeps `max_parallel_workers` at its default of 8, which PostgreSQL accepts above the slot count without a message (the workers come from the free slots, and the small class plans none). The locked `postgresql-18` package (18.6-1.pgdg13+2, checked by its sha256) ships `pg_prewarm.so`. The reading is background work of one process after the database accepts connections: it competes with the first queries for the disk, and it gains the pages the page cache lost while the database slept. A dump holds every valid buffer, not the blocks of one request, and the blocks a prewarm loads are written into the next dump again, so after normal use a dump is as large as the shared buffers (PostgreSQL 18.6, Homebrew, 32 MB of shared buffers: `<<275>>` after a small request, `<<4096>>` after a 130 MB table was read through the buffers and `<<4096>>` again at the next stop, and the next start loaded 3,999 of 4,096 blocks). Every start of such a database reads up to the shared buffers from the disk, in the order of the dump (database, tablespace, relation, block) and not of the first request, and touches that much shared memory at once: 32 MiB for the 256 MiB class and 128 MiB for the 1 GiB class (`tuning`, memory / 8), memory that the page cache could have reclaimed and that the admission rule's `MemAvailable` and the 80 % awake-RAM placement count as used. The live A/B in 11.2 compares a treatment arm with a control arm of the same reboot and decides whether it stays. **Checked against PostgreSQL 18.6.** The library loads without `CREATE EXTENSION`. The leader is a process (`autoprewarm leader`) with no database, so it is no row of `pg_stat_activity`; the per-database worker is one (`autoprewarm worker`, user `postgres`, no state) for as long as it reads. A clean stop writes `autoprewarm.blocks`, 20 bytes a block (4,096 blocks for 32 MB of shared buffers, 16,384 for 128 MB), and nothing is written while the database runs; the next start logs `autoprewarm successfully prewarmed N of M previously-loaded blocks`. The databases are read one at a time: with 2 slots a dump that names two databases loads; with 1 slot the worker cannot register (`registering dynamic bgworker autoprewarm failed`), the leader exits with 1, is started again and still dumps at the next stop. A dump that names a database, a relation, a tablespace, a fork or a block that is gone starts normally (an unknown database ends its worker with a logged `FATAL` and the leader goes on); a dump that is empty, only a header, no dump at all or claims 4 billion blocks makes the leader exit once with an `ERROR`, the database is ready and answers, and the next clean stop writes a valid dump. A hot-standby recovery of a copy of a cluster whose dump names a missing database and relation completes (`archive/recovery.rs`). **Found by the existing tests, fixed.** Four tests that put a database to sleep right after a wake failed with `sql_busy`: the sleep guard counts every `pg_stat_activity` row that has a database and a state other than `idle`, and the autoprewarm worker has a database and no state while it reads (the log query named it: `postgres//autoprewarm worker`). `SLEEP_SQL.GUARD` and the log query `BUSY_SESSIONS` now leave out the `backend_type` `autoprewarm worker`; an autovacuum worker still counts, as before, so a vacuum is not killed by every sleep. The generated `power.generated.json` is regenerated. **Tests.** `cache.rs`: the file set (the places read, the newest WAL segment, user databases only, links and other files left out), the 64-file and 256 MiB bounds with the newest first and an oversize file passed over, a pipe or link that replaces a file after the listing is not opened, a link in place of `global`, `pg_xact`, `pg_wal`, `base`, a database's directory or the data directory lists nothing, the sum over files and `None` where nothing was counted, and on Linux the kernel's own count of a real file (all pages after a read, none after `posix_fadvise` `DONTNEED`, then the whole reading through `host`); `machine/local.rs`: the `mincore` count; `agent/power.rs`: `detail_line` with the six fields (15 of 16, valid for `NodeObservationReport`) and without, the started wake's detail with the cache numbers of the cluster's real files, and a reading that takes 6 s neither holds the wake nor reaches the event; `settings.rs`: the pinned files of both classes, every class's three lines, a worker limit of at least 4 and unchanged `max_connections`, and against a real server the load, the dump at the stop, the reload, and the stale and damaged dumps; `archive/recovery.rs`: the small class's `max_worker_processes` after a recovery is 4, and the restore starts with a stale dump; `sleep.rs`: a worker held by a prepared transaction's lock is no busy session; the proven sleep of `agent/power.rs` finds the dump. The failing tests came first for the settings, the detail line and the guard (the guard test was red with `busy` 1 for the held worker); `cache.rs` was written with its tests and checked by two mutations (reversed order, no byte cap). The whole `pgcf-node-agent` suite also ran on Linux (kernel 6.8, aarch64, a static musl test binary against the image's PostgreSQL 18.6-1.pgdg13+2 build and `nc`): everything passed except the one job test that needs systemd, which fails the same way on main; the `cachestat` test asserts on ext4 and says why it skips on overlayfs; the two real-server tests first failed there because the postmaster starts its background workers just after `ready`, and wait for the leader now. **Measured on the test machine, not acceptance.** Start to ready as the unit measures it (`ready` in `postmaster.pid`), a 307 MB pgbench database, the shared buffers filled by a random read workload before every stop, 15 interleaved runs per arm, PostgreSQL 18.6 (Homebrew) on macOS: 32 MB of shared buffers (the 256 MiB class) 20.7 ms p50 with and 20.7 without autoprewarm, 128 MB (the 1 GiB class) 22.6 and 21.5 ms; the stop that writes the dump 8.0 against 6.7 ms and 11.1 against 9.6 ms (p95 of the 32 MB run is noisy: 45.0 and 30.2 ms ready). On a Linux VM (kernel 6.8, aarch64, the page cache dropped before every start, data on a virtual disk the host caches, so a cold read is cheap there), the image's 18.6-1.pgdg13+2 build, 32 MB of shared buffers, 10 interleaved runs per arm: start to ready 47.0 against 44.9 ms p50, the stop 20.4 against 15.5 ms, and the first request (6 statements over one connection, rows of the workload's hot set) after ready 14.8 ms p50 (p95 16.7) with autoprewarm against 20.6 ms (23.2) without. The start costs about 1–2 ms and the stop 1.5–5 ms; the gain on a cold cache must be confirmed on EU1's disk (11.2). |
| Signed releases on branch `sec-release-signing` (2026-10-19) | Closes the gap that an administrator API key together with write access to the archive bucket could make every node run a binary of their own (the owner's list of 2026-10-19). **Design (8, 9.3).** Ed25519. The private key is the Worker secret `RELEASE_SIGNING_KEY` of the signer `pgcf-release-signer-dev` (`apps/release-signer`): Cloudflare secrets are write-only through the API and the dashboard (the signer's own code can read its binding, so Workers edit access to the account is access to the key), `release-signing.ts provision` makes the pair in memory and sets the secret through the Cloudflare API, and the key is in no GitHub secret, file or terminal (owner decision 2026-10-19: go for release signing, stored in Cloudflare secrets). The public key is `apps/node-agent/release-signing.pub`, which the agent's build reads as the constant `update::RELEASE_KEY` (a file that holds no key stops the build; the repository holds a public key of which nobody has the private half until `provision` replaces it) and which `register-node-image.ts` checks against. The signed text is `signedReleaseManifest`: the line `pgcf-release-manifest/v1`, the release id, the sha256 and size of the four binaries in a fixed order, the Debian snapshot, and the sha256 and size of the package lock (which pins every package and cloudflared). The image is not in it, and the kernel and package digest are derived from the lock. **Signer.** One route, `POST /sign`, no binding but the secret, a tracked `wrangler.jsonc` with no secret and no account id (five variables name the repository, its numeric id and its owner's, the ref and the workflow file). It authenticates the caller by the GitHub Actions OIDC token of its run (RS256 against GitHub's JWKS cached five minutes with one refetch per half minute for an unknown key id; `iss`; `aud` = its own origin; `exp`, `nbf`; `repository`, `repository_id`, `repository_owner`, `repository_owner_id` (GitHub's immutable ids, so that a freed and re-taken name does not pass), `ref` = `refs/heads/main`, `event_name` = `push`, `job_workflow_ref` = `ci.yml` at that ref, `runner_environment` = `github-hosted`), checks the body strictly as `ReleaseManifestFields` (the shared contract: no key beyond the facts), requires the release id to be `sha-<first 12 digits of the token's sha>`, builds the text itself with `signedReleaseManifest` and signs it with WebCrypto Ed25519. It keeps no state: Ed25519 is deterministic, so a second call with the same facts returns the same bytes and there is nothing to count; it does not limit what one token has signed (a valid token gets any digests signed as `sha-<its own commit>` until it expires), and one content per release id is the API's (409 for a different spec of a registered version, to whoever registers second), R2's (no overwrite) and the nodes' (every digest checked) work, with the owner registering the real build first. It logs the release id and a refusal code, never a token, key or digest. **Path.** The job `sign-release` (`needs: [check, node-image]`, a push to `main`, `id-token: write` and `contents: read` and no other permission, no secret, no environment, no cache restored, `pnpm install --ignore-scripts`; the credential for the identity token is in the environment of every step of the job, so the trust root is `ci.yml` on `main`, its pinned actions and the locked dependencies that `node-image` and `sign-release` run) downloads the release artifact by id, runs `sign-release.ts sign` (identity token for the audience `RELEASE_SIGNER_URL`, the manifest's facts to the signer, the answer to `release.sig`; it fails, never skips, when the variable is unset, the signer is unreachable or answers anything but 200 with a signature, or the checked-in public key is still the placeholder; a pull request signs nothing and its dry run says "not signed") and keeps `release.sig` as its own artifact; the registration script checks it with the checkout's key before it uploads a byte (`--release-key` names the old key for the release that rotates it); the API requires a 128-hex signature (400 without one), stores it in the immutable spec (another signature for a registered version is 409), shows it as `spec.signature` and serves it at `/node/v1/releases/<id>/release.sig`; migration `0077` removes the releases registered without one and their region assignments. The desired page is unchanged, so the agents of earlier builds still read it and install the first signed release by its digests. **Agent.** `Service::verified` reads the signature (`Link::fetch_bytes`, at most 256 bytes, 20 s) and verifies it with ring (no new crate) before `install_binaries` and before `install_packages` plans the lock. A release that fails is not downloaded, staged or installed, the node keeps its binaries, and it reports the event `release_refused` with `release_signature_missing` or `release_signature_invalid` (`RELEASE_REFUSALS` in the contracts); a maintenance order for it fails with that class; a signature the agent could not read is tried again and not kept. **Provisioning.** `provision` reads the key file first and refuses a real key without `--rotate`, lists the Worker's secrets by name and refuses an existing `RELEASE_SIGNING_KEY` always, `--rotate` included (a missing Worker is named), then makes the key, sets the secret, and only then replaces the file, atomically; a failure of any step leaves the previous key usable, a file that cannot be written after the API accepted the secret puts the public half (no secret) in the error, and an answer to the secret that never arrives says it is not known whether the Worker holds a new key and names the `wrangler secret delete` that clears the Worker, which held none before. The token and the account id come from variables whose names are on the command line, and the output is the fingerprint of the public key and the next steps. Rotation runs through a second signer (one Worker holds one write-only secret), so the old key signs the transition release and the repository variable moves afterwards. **Tests, written before the code.** Run red first (`ReleaseManifestFields` and the signer's modules did not exist; the provisioning and signing scripts' tests failed on the old commands). Contracts: `release-signing.test.ts` (7: the exact manifest text, what a signature covers and what it leaves out, ambiguous values refused, the registration's signature shape, the spec and the file name, the two classes, the strict fields of a signer). Signer, in workerd with a key and an RSA JWKS made at test time (35): the signature verifies over the text the agent builds, written out by hand, and is the same every time; a release of another commit, another name, any extra or missing or malformed field, a body over 8 KiB and a wrong content type are refused; 22 token rejections (audience, issuer, repository, owner, the repository's and the owner's numeric id, a missing id, an id as a number, ref, pull-request ref, event, dispatch, three workflow files, runner, commit), the time rules with the skew, 13 malformed or forged tokens (algorithm, key id, another key under the same id, another payload, unsecured), the key cache and the one refetch for an unknown key id, GitHub unreachable, redirecting or answering rubbish giving 503, a missing or malformed key or variable (the two ids included) giving 500, one route, and the logs holding the release id and no token, key or digest; mutation checks (no event, algorithm, audience or commit check) each turned a test red. Scripts: `release-signing.test.ts` (8, against a Cloudflare API on the loopback interface: the order, which shows the file still held the placeholder while the API was asked to set the secret; a refused secret leaves the file and echoes neither token nor key; an existing secret refused with and without `--rotate` after the one request that lists the secrets (no PUT, the file and the secrets as they were), a real key refused without `--rotate`, and the rotation to a Worker that holds none; an answer to the secret that never arrives; a missing Worker; a file that cannot be written; the variable names; the API base), `sign-release.test.ts` (5, against a stand-in token endpoint and signer: the audience and the bearer credentials, the exact facts, the file's mode; the placeholder asks nobody; the missing variables; a refusal, an unreachable signer and rubbish answers, none of which leaves a file or shows a token; no redirect and the address rules), `scripts/ci/release-signing-workflow.test.ts` (4: no secret, no environment, `needs: [check, node-image]`, no permission but the checkout's and the token's, no cache, `id-token: write` only on the signing job and the dispatch-only `external_probe` whose token the signer refuses, no step that could pass over the signing, and the signer's variables equal this workflow) and the registration script's test of a signature present, missing, malformed, another release's and another key's. API: `fleet-releases.test.ts`, `release-files.test.ts` and `openapi.test.ts` as before. Node agent, with the shared vector `releases` (every digest different): `update::tests` (8) and in `service::tests` an unsigned and a forged release refused with nothing downloaded and a signed one installed after them, and a maintenance order of a forged release failed with the class and no lock read. Runbook: [docs/operations/release-signing.md](../operations/release-signing.md) (deploying the signer, provisioning, the merge precondition, rotation, loss). **Reviews of 2026-10-19 (independent, Opus).** The first found and had fixed: the headline claim overstated the protection (it covers the update path of running nodes, not the install path); a key set before the commit with its public half reached `main` would have signed a placeholder build; the migration is `0077` because the audit branch owns `0076`; the file route documents the `text/plain` answer. Both reviews found that the first design kept the key where GitHub could hand it out (a repository secret, then an environment secret, which any workflow of a branch the policy admits can read) and that the one-line first-key command could destroy the only copy of the private key (a failed first process of `keygen | gh secret set` sets an empty secret over it). The signer, `provision` and the job without a secret replace both. The review of that design (independent, Opus) found and had fixed: `--rotate` also lifted the refusal for an existing secret, so the runbook's rotation command without its `--worker` flag would have overwritten the only copy of the key at the live signer (`provision` now never replaces a secret; `--rotate` lifts only the check of the committed key); `sign-release` signed a commit whose `check` job had failed (it now needs `check` as well); the claim that only `sign-release.ts` runs with the token's credential was false, because `id-token: write` puts it in the environment of every step (the trust root is now stated as `ci.yml` on `main`, its pinned actions and the locked dependencies, the job restores no cache and has no `actions: read`); two statements overstated what is protected (a valid token gets any digests signed as its own commit's release until it expires, and Workers edit access to the account can read the key out of the signer, so a suspected misuse is treated as a key loss); the signer checked the repository and its owner by name only, which a rename, a transfer or a deletion frees for someone else (it checks GitHub's numeric ids as well); and one contract test only compared two constants with their own literals (deleted). A third review (independent, Opus) confirmed those fixes in code and found three wording errors, fixed: the runbook and a signer test comment said the API's 409 goes to whoever registers first (the first registration stands and a later, different one gets the 409), the build section still said `sign-release` needs `node-image` alone, and the runbook gave a token's lifetime as the window of code that runs in the job, which can keep minting tokens until the job ends. **Not covered.** The install and reinstall path of a node (the installer checks the image's sha256 only, from a spec an administrator wrote; open, 11.2), a rollback to an older signed release (an administrator can still assign it), a loss of the secret (no backup by design: every node is reinstalled), and the trust root: whoever can push to `main`, change `ci.yml` there or get code into a locked dependency that `node-image` or `sign-release` runs, and whoever has Workers edit access to the account (who can read the key out of the signer). |
| Admin accountability on branch `sec-admin-audit` (2026-10-19) | **Read-only scope.** `ApiKeyScope` gains `admin_read` (migration `0076`; such a key has no project). It reads what `admin` reads and changes nothing: `getAuth` refuses every request of an `admin_read` key that is not a GET or HEAD with 403 before a handler runs, and only after the attempt is in the audit log. The guard has to sit there and not in each route: a handler that tests only "not an integrator" lets the key through, and with the guard removed the read-only key created a database (`POST /v1/databases`, 202). `requireScope(c, "admin")` still matches `admin` exactly, so every route that required it still does; the 24 GET routes that expose operational data (the nodes and their inventory, regions, fleet releases, health, costs, node diagnostics, maintenance, loss and addition reads, capacity policy and decision, node policy, resource profile rollout, infrastructure backups, moves and the key list) call the new `requireAdminRead`. `GET /v1/regions` shows both scopes the full records, the probe routes let `admin_read` read the answers but not ask, and the connection URI carries a password for an integrator only, as before. The served OpenAPI says so per route (`admin_read` as a documentation auth kind, 403 documented on every write route an integrator can reach; `API_CONTRACT_VERSION` 1.3.0). **Expiry.** `api_keys.expires_at`, set at creation (`ApiKeyCreate.expires_at`, a canonical future timestamp), shown in `ApiKey`, enforced in `getAuth` like a revocation (401). Default, in the contract and in `docs/operations/credentials.md`: an `admin` or `admin_read` key that an administrator key makes expires 90 days after its creation and may ask for at most 366 days; an integrator key never expires unless it asks; the first key (bootstrap token) never expires unless it asks, because the bootstrap works once and a first key that lapsed could not be replaced through the API. Keys from before `0076` have no expiry. **Audit log.** `audit_events` and `GET /v1/audit-events` (both administrator scopes, cursor pagination, newest first). A request of an `admin` or `admin_read` key that is not a GET or HEAD is written **before** it runs (key ID, scope, method, route template such as `/v1/api-keys/{id}`, request ID, time, status null) and its status is added by a middleware after the answer; a refusal (401) of a revoked or an expired administrator key that was presented with its real secret is written with 401. No body, query, path parameter, key material or address. **Fail closed:** a change cannot be taken back, so a row written after it could be lost with the change made; a row that cannot be written answers 500 and nothing runs (the test puts a trigger that aborts every insert on the real D1 table: the project was not created, reads and refusals still worked, and the same request ran once the trigger was gone). A status that cannot be written is logged and the answer stands; the row keeps `status` null, which the contract defines as unknown, never success. A wrong secret, a key that names no key row and an integrator's key leave no row, so that anyone who can send a request cannot grow the table: the lookup identifier is not secret (it is inside every key string and listed to every `admin_read` holder), and the first version recorded a refusal whenever it matched an administrator row, which let anyone write an INSERT and a retention DELETE per request, for rows that revoked keys still match. **Retention differs from the task:** the 90 days are not a step of the minute cron. `usage-cron.test.ts` and `admission-cron-bounds.test.ts` hold the whole cron to 850 statements and it is at 850; the `audit_purge` step first built failed both with 851 (the wall SEC-09 met for probes), so it was removed. An event older than 90 days is never listed (the query filters on it), and the next row written, a change or a refused administrator key, deletes the older ones in a statement of its own whose failure is logged and does not stop the request. **Migration `0076`** rebuilds `api_keys` for the wider `scope` CHECK as `0017` rebuilt `node_additions` (`PRAGMA defer_foreign_keys`, new table filled, old one dropped, new one renamed to `api_keys`, so that the foreign key of `idempotency_keys` keeps naming `api_keys`; renaming the old table away would repoint it). Rehearsed on a SQLite 3.51 database built from `0001`–`0075` that held a key and an idempotency record, foreign keys on, in one transaction: it committed, `foreign_key_check` was empty, both rows were kept and the foreign key still names `api_keys`. A test inserts an orphan idempotency record and gets the foreign key error, and a duplicate lookup identifier, an `admin_read` key with a project and a scope `root` are each refused. **Tests written first and failing:** all 13 tests of the first `admin-accountability.test.ts` failed against the unchanged tree (no `admin_read`, no `audit_events`), then passed; the retention tests were rewritten when the cron step was removed (14 in all). Mutations that fail them: the read-only guard removed, the retention filter and the deletion removed. Locally green: contracts 216 tests in 18 files with the generator checks (generated files unchanged, so no Rust consumer changed), API 962 tests in 103 files (two new OpenAPI tests; the role-lifecycle one now expects 403 on `DELETE …/roles/{name}`; one full run while `tsc` and `prettier` ran beside it counted 851 statements in `admission-cron-bounds.test.ts`, which sits at the bound: alone it counts 850 and 849, and the next full run with nothing beside it passed all 960; 962 after the review fixes below), typecheck of every package and of `scripts/ci` and `scripts/operations`, eslint, prettier, plan privacy and Mermaider. **Review fixes (an independent review, checked against the code; six findings, all real).** (1) *Anyone could write audit rows.* `getAuth` recorded a refusal whenever an administrator row matched the lookup identifier, even with a wrong secret, and the identifier is public. It now records only when the secret matched (`row && matches && isAdminScope`): the test that expected a third row for a wrong secret failed first (three rows, two expected), then passed. (2) *OpenAPI.* `POST /v1/nodes/{id}/diagnostics` answers 403 to an `admin_read` key and the document did not say so; a new test walks every `/v1` operation that is not a read and requires 403 in its responses: it failed with exactly that one operation (the same check over `publicRouteErrors` found no other), and passes with 403 added there and in the route description. (3) The `asks` parameter of `administeredNode` was dead (`getAuth` refuses `admin_read` before any POST handler runs) and is gone; the test of write attempts now includes that POST, and the read of the list by an `admin_read` key still answers 200. (4) *Lockout after expiry.* Confirmed in `createApiKey`: the bootstrap insert needs that no `admin` row exists, a lapsed or revoked row counts, and every key an administrator makes lapses within 366 days. `docs/operations/credentials.md` gains Lapsed administrator keys (an `expires_at` edit in the D1 console, which the audit log does not record) and the 11.2 row sends the operator there before the old admin key is revoked. No warning or header is added: the key list already shows `expires_at`. (5) *The fail-closed test never ran the refusal path.* It presented a key with no row. It now presents a revoked administrator key with its real secret while the insert trigger is in place and expects 401 with no row written; with the catch of `auditRefusedKey` replaced by a rethrow it fails (500 instead of 401), with the code as built it passes. (6) The key section of `integrator-handover.md` said two scopes and then named a third; it says three. **Not run:** the live checks (11.2) and the node agent's kernel test, because nothing in Rust or in the generated vectors changed |
| Stage 6 documents rewritten for the node runtime, ahead of Stage 5, on branch `docs-stage6` (2026-10-19) | The README, the operator runbooks (`operator-installation`, `recovery`, `credentials`, `infrastructure-backups`, `native-external-probe`), the integrator handover, THIRD_PARTY.md, `infra/README.md`, `infra/node-image/README.md`, the Mermaider README and `parallel-wake-ticket.md` no longer describe the Kubernetes runtime, the slot pool, the regional controller, thin quotas, CloudNativePG schedules, the old placement settings or per-region agent keys as current. New: `apps/node-agent/README.md`, and the runbook sections for the region's node policy, the capacity policy, adding and reinstalling a node, node credentials, fencing and the reinstall guard, and the full pool and full volume; the handover states every G11 item, with the numbers that are only starting values or first medians marked as such. The statements about routes, contracts, placement, node capacity, node loss, the node agent and the gateway were read against the code before they were kept; every number kept is a plan row's, quoted as that row gives it (series, sample size and sleep time), or the code's, except two sets of figures that no plan row holds: the round-trip figures behind the estimates of handover 3.8, which it marks as earlier measurements that were not repeated, and the usage-sample gaps of the retired runtime's nightly D1 exports in `infrastructure-backups.md` (gaps of 37 to 72 s in four of six runs), which come from the runbook table that `93b18d5b` deleted (Leftovers (9) in 11.2).
