A second review confirmed those fixes and found five more, each checked against the code and fixed: the handover said G5 lacked a runbook action for some kinds (all 19 kinds carry one in `INFRA_ALERT_ACTIONS`; G5 is met on the receiver side and waits for the owner to confirm the recipient), it read as if a second node existed in the region (there is one until EU2 is approved), it said storage growth is refused only past the logical cap (`growthFits` also refuses an offline node and a pool that is unobserved or has no room), the node agent's README named `bin/current` in `update.rs` alone (`main.rs`, `machine/node.rs` and two image units name it too), and this row's list of exceptions was incomplete. **Not verified:** the live installation's node policy, capacity policy and class catalog (named, not stated); the full pool and full volume on EU1 (5.5); the route keyring and R2 parent-key rotation (read from the code, never run); the Debian package licenses of e2fsprogs, nftables, OpenSSH and Python (from package knowledge, not the image's copyright files); no Rust or TypeScript suite and no CI run on this branch. Checks that ran: 303 relative links and the named repository paths resolve (one is a test the proposal names for the future), tables are well-formed, `prettier --check .` is green (it skips Markdown and `infra`), Mermaider `update` then `check`. Stage 6 is not closed: see 11.2 |
| G6 restore tool and the sleeping-hour traffic rollup, on branch `g6-restore-tool` (2026-10-19) | **G6 tool.** `scripts/operations/restore-infrastructure-backup.ts` (`decrypt` and `import`; runbook: the restore drill in `docs/operations/infrastructure-backups.md`) opens a `PGCFINF1` artifact with the offline keyring (an environment variable's name, never an argument; the key version the artifact names, not the active one), authenticates the GCM tag, the AAD and the plaintext hash and length, holds the artifact to the API receipt when given one, and imports it into a new, empty scratch D1 database addressed by ID through `wrangler d1 execute --remote --file` from a directory with no wrangler configuration. It refuses the database the artifact was exported from and any database that holds a schema object. The decrypted file exists only as `0600` in a `0700` directory; SIGINT, SIGTERM and SIGHUP stop wrangler, remove it and end the run with exit code 128 plus the signal number (SIGKILL cannot be caught; the runbook says what to delete). It prints the day, byte counts, hashes, table counts and the scratch database's ID, and no control database ID, SQL, key or wrangler text. **Tests** encrypt with the Worker's own `encryptSource` and cover a flipped byte, a cut file, a changed identity, another key and a missing key id; a wrangler double backed by real SQLite (the table count is what SQLite answers: the first version counted indexes, triggers and views as tables: 140 for 55 tables on that import); and a real SIGINT and SIGTERM sent to the CLI child while the double holds the import (10 tests; 73 in `scripts/ci` and `scripts/operations`). **Found: the raw D1 export does not import.** The wrangler local export of the repository's 76 migrations (wrangler 4.145: `d1 migrations apply --local`, then `d1 export --local`; 55 tables, 78 `INSERT` statements (76 `d1_migrations` rows, one `infrastructure_backup_config` row, one `sqlite_sequence` row), 79,426 bytes) fails with `no such table: main.regions`: `regions` is created at line 626, after `nodes` (127) and `infrastructure_backup_config` (511), and the first row of a table that references it meets a missing parent, also under `PRAGMA defer_foreign_keys`. With every `CREATE TABLE` ahead of the rest (`schemaFirst`, every statement byte for byte) it imports in 220 statements (`d1 execute --local --file`), a second export has the same sorted lines and `PRAGMA foreign_key_check` is empty. A synthetic 323 MB dump reorders in 5.5 s with 257 MiB resident (laptop, `/usr/bin/time -l`). **Not verified:** the provider's own export and import, and that D1 runs the file as one transaction (the reordered file fails statement by statement with `FOREIGN KEY constraint failed`); the drill is where they are first tried. **G7 defect fixed.** The node emits no row for a sleeping database, but `computeUsageHour` counted asleep time among the spans that need gateway coverage, so every hour that held sleep time, and every hour asleep from start to end, had `null` ingress, egress, connections and connection time with the gap `traffic_coverage`. Only awake time needs coverage now; an asleep span without a row is measured zero, and traffic counted against a sleeping database (a refused wake) is added. Tests first (red: 3 of the 4 new cases, an hour asleep answered `ingress_bytes` null; green: the 24 cases of `usage.test.ts`; one case keeps awake time without a row a gap); two older cases that used a never-ready database as the gap became awake ones. Hours finalized before the deploy keep `null` traffic. How the lifecycle events' times line up with the agent's one-minute row boundaries in an hour that holds a wake or a sleep is not measured |
| Keep-warm for the DatabaseActor measured on scratch Workers, branch `keepwarm-measure` (2026-10-19; D14 part a) | **Method.** Scratch Workers on the Dev account (`pgcf-kw-scratch-*`), deleted afterwards and checked by listing: 79 Workers scripts and 18 Durable Object namespaces before and after, none of the prefix left, the product Workers untouched. A caller Worker (colo AMS, where the laptop is routed; the objects answered from AMS) made the Edge's call: `idFromName`, hint `weur`, one RPC. Two hosts: a minimal SQLite-backed class with the SQL of an admission (2.4 KB) and the real `DatabaseActor` of main `1987beb7` (1.11 MB minified), primed with an idle-sleeper snapshot so that `admitWake` issues a ticket. A sample is one admission in a fresh object; the latency is the caller's clock around the call; the object reports its constructor and module ids (a new constructor with the same module is a hibernation resume, a new module a reloaded isolate). Variants were interleaved per iteration, one object of each probed in the same second; differences are medians of paired differences with 95 % bootstrap intervals; 48 samples per cell in Q1 and in the extension, 60 in Q2 and Q3. **Q1, idle gap (real actor, p50 / p95 ms; share of probes that found a reloaded module).** 3 s: 25 / 45, 0 %; 8 s: 21 / 68, 0 %; 15 s: 34 / 289, 19 %; 30 s: 30 / 294, 29 %; 60 s: 40 / 341, 52 %; 120 s: 141 / 330, 71 %; 300 s: 167 / 424, 81 % (max 927). Minimal class: 24 / 40, 22 / 49, 24 / 74, 29 / 85, 30 / 69, 29 / 194 and 47 / 125 ms, with 0, 0, 19, 29, 50, 56 and 73 % reloaded. Up to 8 s the object stays in memory; after 10 s it hibernates, and the next call re-runs the constructor with the module still loaded (21–32 ms at p50 up to a gap of 120 s, no step). The step is the eviction of the isolate (p50 158–215 ms real; 38–58 ms minimal where another isolate had the module), and it has no threshold: its share rises with the gap. With no activity at all in the class (the Q2 control, idle minutes) every probe was cold: 256 / 447 ms real (max 1,109) and 106 / 327 ms minimal (the 105 ms floor of 2026-10-18). Q1 ran in a class that the experiment itself kept busy, so its shares are lower bounds of cold. **Q2 and Q3, does a keep-warm work (real actor, p50 / p95 ms; probes at or above 100 ms; paired difference to the control with its 95 % interval).** None 256 / 447, 98 %. Alarm that re-arms itself every 4 s: 46 / 75, 5 % (−214 ms, −244..−138); every 8 s: 43 / 76, 3 %; every 30 s: 44 / 248, 20 %; every 60 s: 48 / 204, 28 % (−144, −216..−123). Cron trigger that visits every minute: 48 / 300, 33 % (−146, −200..−120). Visit loop every 5 s: 40 / 61, 3 %; every 10 s: 38 / 58, 3 % (−210, −264..−145). Extension run (control 279 / 513): visit every 15 s 46 / 252, 10 %; every 20 s 45 / 249, 17 %; every 30 s 48 / 317, 23 %. **Yes, if it comes every 10 s or faster:** every probe was fast (3–5 % at or above 100 ms) and none found a new constructor (0 of 60 at alarm 4 s and 8 s and at visit 5 s and 10 s), because a random connect arrives less than 10 s after the last touch; the visit logs show the edge of hibernation (a new constructor in 0.1 % of the visits at 5 s and in 40 % at 10 s). Beyond 10 s the object hibernates (every visit then finds a new constructor) and its isolate is evicted between visits: share of visits that found a reloaded module with 60 objects per cell, 15 s 23 % (5,280 visits), 30 s 42 % (2,640), 60 s 78 % (1,320); with 20 objects at 30 s 39 % (880). The probes see it as a tail, not as a shift (p50 stays near 45 ms, p95 250–317 ms), and the time since the last visit decides: cron, real actor, 0–10 s after a visit 45 / 64 ms, 50–61 s after 171 / 300 ms (n=11). **Q4, billed (analytics over 37 minutes, 20 objects per variant, each variant in its own namespace; extrapolated to 1,000 databases for 30 days at the pricing page last updated 2026-10-18: USD 0.15 per million requests with 1 million included, USD 12.50 per million GB-s with 400,000 included, USD 1.00 per million rows written with 50 million included and each `setAlarm()` one row, USD 0.001 per million rows read).** `duration` is GB-s at 0.128 GB per object-second, checked against a resident control. Gross per month, with the figure after the included allowances in brackets (if nothing else on the account uses them): no keep-warm USD 0; alarm 4 s USD 775 (719); alarm 8 s USD 386 (331); alarm 30 s USD 103 (50); alarm 60 s USD 51 (6); cron visit every 60 s USD 17.5 (12.3); visit every 5 s USD 83.8 (78.6); every 10 s USD 43.6 (38.7); every 15 s USD 29.9 (25.8); every 20 s USD 25.2 (20.0); every 30 s USD 16.3 (12.8). The rows dominate the alarms: a `setAlarm()` row costs 6.7 times a request, and at 4 s the bill is USD 98 requests, 26 duration and 651 rows. The analytics counts 0 rows written for the alarms while the pricing page bills each `setAlarm()` as one, so both are given and the invoice was not read. **No variant keeps an object resident all month:** the cadences bill 0.04–0.62 % of resident time (0.16–2.85 GB-s per object-hour, USD 1.5–26 duration per 1,000 databases per month, USD 11 on the cron). An object held resident on purpose (a pending timer and an alarm every 30 s, 5 objects) billed 99.2 % of the time, 457 GB-s per object-hour: 329 million GB-s, USD 4,114 per 1,000 databases per month for duration alone. Storage is the same in every variant (the pricing page's 12 KB per empty object, about USD 0.003 per 1,000; the analytics returned no storage rows). Workers Paid is inferred, since the subscription API refuses the token: 342,598 Durable Object requests by 11:00 UTC on 2026-10-19 with no quota error (Workers Free stops at 100,000 a day), and `default_usage_model` is `standard`. **Limits.** One client and one colo; the Edge's own colo was not observed. Objects were created minutes before their probe. 60 objects per class; thousands may behave differently. 5–18 samples per time-since-visit bin. Five objects probed again and again at 30 s never reloaded their isolate (48 probes), while the visit logs of five objects did (68 % of 220 visits); the reason is unknown, so the five-object cell is not used. The cron here visits with a no-op RPC, 160 objects per tick; production's cron visits 200 databases per page with a D1 and actor write, so each database every N/200 minutes, which is no keep-warm for N above 200. A new cron schedule needed 9–10 minutes before its first tick (the page says up to 15). The analytics lagged about five minutes. A visitor object with an alarm every 8–10 s over about 200 actors was not measured. **Q5, Workers KV, not run:** the Dev token `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN_PGCF_DEV_30D` has no Workers KV Storage permission (list and create answer `Authentication error`, code 10000), so no KV namespace was made. It needs `Workers KV Storage: Edit`; the cold KV read p50 / p95 and the staleness window are not known. |
| Node observation reports no longer outrun the agent's timeout, on branch `observation-scale` (2026-10-19) | **Found live.** EU1 with about 235 databases posted observations that timed out for hours (`observation_not_posted`, class `http_timeout`, the agent's 20 s timeout), five creates stood `pending` for 30–60 minutes and the 300-database population stalled. **Cloudflare, not Contabo:** Workers Logs held 70 `POST /node/v1/observations` in an hour (the agent's attempts: every one arrived), the handlers ran 50–65 s (log lines of one invocation 64 s apart), the same node's desired-page reads answered in 0.3 s and a SQL through the Edge in 2.3 s, and the timeouts began before the firewall change of 11:18 UTC. **Cause:** `nodeObservations()` took the up to 200 databases of a report one after another, and each changed one waited up to 1 s for its own cold DatabaseActor's publish. **Fix:** the API takes eight at a time with a 12 s budget (a report with databases left unstarted is refused whole with the existing retryable 503, which both agent releases keep and re-post), the agent sends 64 per report with what is not asleep first and halves the batch after a timeout or 5xx, and a `node_observation_report` line logs every report that took 2 s or held 50 databases. Test first: 48 publishes of 200 ms took 10.2 s sequentially and 1.4 s with eight in flight. **Live after the API deploy (agent half still to release):** the first report held 200 observations and took 10.2 s (slowest 1.1 s, all accepted), the next 31 took 2.7 s, the backlog was gone within 13 s and the stuck creates completed. Reviewed by Opus: concurrency has no race (unique ids per report, per-database admission locks), the budget never drops an observation silently |

| Volumes out of PID 1's mount namespace (`MountImages=`) on branch `sandbox-v4h` (2026-10-19) | **What.** The agent unit has `PrivateMounts=yes`, so the volumes it mounts are in its own mount namespace. A database unit mounts its own volume (`MountImages=/run/pgcf/dev/<id>:/var/lib/pgcf/db/<id>:root:nosuid,nodev,noexec,noatime`) from `/run/pgcf/dev/<id>`, a symlink that the agent keeps in a directory only root can use (0700, checked at every use) and that the unit asserts (`AssertPathExists=`): it is made by rename after the volume is attached, removed first of all before an unmount, a repair or a removal, removed when the pool's failure marks the volume suspect, and removed for every volume at the agent's start. Jobs mount the device of the generation they serve (a restore its target's). The image masks `modprobe@loop`, `@dm_mod` and `@dm_verity`, which `MountImages=` would run again for every start. **Why.** systemd's executor copies PID 1's mount namespace for every unit it starts and reads its mount table about 64 times (ftrace: 56 scans in the V4h unit against 3 for a plain unit), so the start of `pgcf-pg@` grew with the volumes mounted there, and PID 1 used 1,070 ms of CPU to mount 1,000 volumes where it used 0 ms when the agent's namespace does it. **Lab table** (median ms of 20 to 25 runs per cell after 2 warm-up rounds, rounds interleaved; N is the number of volumes present; the host has 21 base mounts; ext4 on loop devices unless marked; the unit's ExecStart is a static stand-in binary, not PostgreSQL). Executor handoff (exec of the executor to exec of ExecStart) at N = 17 / 164 / 300 / 1,000: volumes in PID 1's namespace 36.5 / 57.5 / 80.8 / 243.1 (p95 at 1,000: 496); volumes in the agent's namespace with the unit mounting its own 36.2 / 39.2 / 34.7 / 39.8; on device-mapper thin volumes (calm rerun) 34.8 / 63.6 / 89.3 / 272.5 against 35.8 / 35.4 / 40.1 / 35.5. `systemctl start` to ExecStart at the same counts: 50.4 / 65.9 / 88.8 / 234.5 against 52.8 / 55.0 / 52.0 / 53.8. A backup job's pattern (a tmpfs over `/var/lib/pgcf` and a bind of the volume) 54.7 / 114.6 / 259.0 / 2,150.7, which grows faster than linearly (about with the square of N), against 54.0 at 164 and 55.5 at 1,000 with `MountImages=`. Running units do not slow a start: 55.3 / 53.3 / 51.1 / 56.0 at 0 / 164 / 300 / 900 awake units (1,000 volumes), against 286.9 (none awake) and 261.7 (300 awake) with the volumes in PID 1's namespace. **Caveats.** One Debian 13 container with systemd 257.13 as PID 1 in a laptop VM (Ubuntu 24.04, kernel 6.8, 6 vCPU, Apple silicon), shared with other workloads (every cell has a plain unit as a noise control); not EU1's kernel (6.12) or hardware; small volumes; the start path only, no data-plane or I/O effect measured. **The 6.6 times is unverified.** EU1's executor handoff before the change was 105 ms at 17 and 245 ms at 164 volumes, about 1 ms per volume, which is 6.6 times the lab's slope between those two counts (0.14 ms per volume) and 4 to 5 times its four-point slope; the cause of the factor is not found (kernel, hardware, the node's other mounts and the lab's devices are candidates), so only the shapes transfer (linear in N with the volumes in PID 1's namespace, flat with them in the agent's) and the milliseconds do not. The effect on EU1 is not measured (11.2). **The device policy is wider.** `MountImages=` adds `block-device-mapper`, `block-blkext` and `block-loop` (`rwm`) and `/dev/mapper/control` and `/dev/loop-control` (`rw`) to the unit's `DeviceAllow`, next to the `char-rtc r` it had; `systemctl show -p DeviceAllow` lists exactly these six, the units' lab and the boot test assert it, and it cannot be avoided while `MountImages=` is used. What neutralises it, checked as the unit's user in the lab: the unit's private `/dev` has no block node, `mknod` fails, and a device-mapper node by its number and the volume's device by its name cannot be opened (the probe fails with 35 block nodes when `PrivateDevices=` is turned off); behind that stand the empty capability set, `NoNewPrivileges=yes` and the nodes being `root:disk 0660`. **What stays visible.** The ids and generations of every database (`/etc/passwd`, `/run/pgcf`, `/sys/block/dm-*/dm/name`), a neighbour's I/O and write counters (`/sys/block/dm-*/stat`, `/sys/fs/ext4/dm-*`) and its `memory.current` and `cgroup.procs` (`/sys/fs/cgroup/pgcf.slice/pgcf-pg@<id>.service`) stay readable through sysfs and the cgroup tree, as before; the isolation keeps data and volumes out of reach and does not hide other databases. **A volume made before the gap.** Every filesystem is now made and grown 4 MiB (one extent) before the end of its volume, because image dissection refuses a device that carries a RAID or ZFS signature where libblkid probes, and the farthest probe (`promise_raid`) reads 3,087 sectors (1,580,544 bytes) from the end (lab, libblkid 2.41.5: a Promise signature there made `blkid -p` call an ext4 device a RAID member, and one sector farther from the end it did not), so tenant data can never form one there. The risk accepted for Dev: a volume made before the gap, which is every volume on EU1 today, fills its volume and has no such protection until it grows, so a tenant that knew the prober could make it refuse its mount and its database could neither start nor archive. The migration is to recreate the databases, or to shrink the filesystem offline while the database sleeps (`e2fsck -f`, then `resize2fs <device> <volume size - 4 MiB>K`); the reinstall that brings the image to a node (11.2) makes every volume again, with the gap. A grow to the size a filesystem already has is skipped, because `resize2fs` refuses to shrink one for ever (lab: "On-line shrinking not supported"). |
| Volume sandbox: review fixes and the rule for a held volume, on branch `sandbox-v4h` (2026-10-19) | An independent review of the commit above found 11 things, and a second review of the fixes found a data-safety gap, a test double that did not follow the node, and rows that did not reach the plan; each was checked in the code, and each real one was fixed with its failing test first. **The agent's mount table is no witness.** The agent's namespace ends with the agent, and a unit or a job mounts a volume in a namespace of its own, so a test double that reported every volume mounted for ever hid the places that read it. The double now follows the node: its mounts and links end with an agent restart, a unit keeps the volume it started on, a start without the link fails its assertion, and an unmount, a removal (lvm2: "in use") and a repair are `Busy` while a running unit or a job holds the volume (`hold_volume` and `release_volume` stand for a job with `MountImages=`), and the next generation is neither made nor mounted then (`OtherGeneration`). (1) A restarted agent with no record of a database stops the unit of a superseded generation by the unit's own `/proc/<pid>/mountinfo` (`Machine::unit_volume`); an answer that cannot be read leaves the order `unit_not_stopped`. (2) The pool's failure tags every volume whose device is open (`lv_attr` `o`), and ext4's errors are read from `/sys/fs/ext4/dm-<minor>`, wherever the filesystem is mounted. (3) The detach looks for ext4's entry after the agent's own unmount (waits 250 ms; the entry went 1 to 2 ms after a holder ended, 20 runs), mounts the volume again and answers `Busy` if it stays; `debugfs -w` writes a filesystem that another namespace has mounted where `e2fsck` stops with 8 (lab), so no repair starts on a held volume. (4) The 4 MiB gap (above). (5) `resize2fs` is skipped for a filesystem that has the size. (6) The lock split of `mount_all` and its fairness test are deleted (no observed problem). Lab, 300 volumes of 32 MiB, serial under the storage lock: `mount_all` of 50 / 100 / 300 volumes took 0.20 / 0.26 / 0.86 s at first and 0.17 / 0.30 / 0.89 s after the rule below (about 3 ms each), and unmounting 300 took 1.1 s. (7) The rollout order is in 11.2. (8) A wake and a recovery ask whether the volume is attached (the link), not whether its directory is a mount. (9) `pgcf-agent check` refuses to run in PID 1's namespace. (10) The unit's user finds no block node in `/dev` and cannot make or open one (lab and boot test). (11) The job-device test is a plain test. **The rule for a held volume (data safety, found by the second review).** After an agent restart the boot's `mount_all` mounts only the generation the page names, and the detach, the `create_volume` check, the repair and the removal each looked at the agent's own mount table, so a unit that still ran on the old generation was invisible: the next generation could be built, mounted and linked, and a later start of `pgcf-pg@<id>` would have been a no-op on the old unit (systemd leaves a running unit as it is), so the database would have gone on running on the old volume while the agent believed it ran on the new one. Reproduced in the storage lab on the old logic: `unmount_volume` and `create_volume` answered Ok and the new volume was built, mounted and linked while a unit held the old one. The rule, stated in Appendix C and the node-image README: a volume is detached, and a generation of a database is made or mounted, only while no filesystem of that volume (for a detach) or of another generation of the same database (for a make or a mount) is alive in any mount namespace, which ext4's `/sys/fs/ext4/dm-<minor>` entry says (it exists while a superblock does), with or without a mount of the agent's own. A held volume answers `Busy`, a held other generation `OtherGeneration`, and lvm2's own refusal of an open device is the removal's `Busy`. `attach` is the one place that mounts a generation (create, boot, wake, recovery, grow), so the check is there, and in `create_volume` before anything is made. The build releases the previous generation before it writes anything, and ends `build_failed` when it cannot, leaving the record of the old generation as it was, so the next pull releases it again; before, the build went on, wrote the new generation's record, and the retry never released the old volume again (red in the agent test with the faithful double). The plan step does not forbid a build while a unit runs, because a build that a crash stopped resumes with its own recovery unit running on the new generation; the kernel rule needs no such exception. **Tests.** Failing first: three unit tests of the rule on a recorded `lvs` (detach, create, attach); the storage lab test of the restart with the old generation held only by a unit (red on the old logic because the boot mounted the next generation; now `mount_all`, `mount_volume` and `create_volume` refuse, `unmount_volume`, `recover_volume` and `remove_volume` answer `Busy`, the unit's own view of its data is unchanged, and everything goes through once the unit has stopped); the node lab on real systemd and LVM (red on the old logic with `Ok(())` for the unmount); an agent test in which a move target is not built while the unit of the source runs after a restart and is built once it has stopped (red on the old logic: the record was overwritten and the retry never released the volume). The Busy paths of the agent run against the faithful double: a release followed by a build, a recovery that tries again until a job lets go, and a superseded order that ends `volume_not_removed` and is followed by one that removes the volume. `testpg::bin_directory` now fails when there is no PostgreSQL 18 and `CI` is set, instead of letting the tests that need one skip: the Rust step of CI runs `cargo test --workspace` with the pinned PostgreSQL 18.6, which is where the agent's tests, the new ones included, run (red against the old behaviour in a unit test of the guard); the job-device test needs no database. Gates: the contracts checks, typecheck, eslint, prettier, plan privacy, the image's units tests (14), fmt, clippy with and without the lab cfg, 518 lib tests (511 at the merged HEAD, 13 ignored) in the pinned builder image with PostgreSQL 18.6, contrib (`pg_prewarm`) and `ps` as a non-root user, the storage lab (28 tests), the systemd lab (12: units 10, node 1, job 1) and Mermaider. The storage lab's full-pool test depends on writeback timing: under CPU load it failed 3 of 6 runs both at the commit before and after the first round, and passed in every quiet run. |
| Storage samples every minute and alert IDs that name the error, on branch `sample-ids` (2026-10-19) | **Found live.** In a volume-full run the volume filled in three minutes while `storage` still showed the 15 % of the last sample: `nodeVolumes` stored one sample per database per quarter hour (sequence `floor(at / 15 min)`), so `health.disk`, `storage` and the usage rollup's `storage_used_bytes_max` lagged by up to 15 minutes. **Read first, then recorded:** the alerts `database_volume_warning` and `database_volume_critical` never read those samples; they read `node_volumes`, the node's newest report (posted every 60 s, at most 180 s old). The new alert test through `nodeVolumes` and `runInfrastructureHealthAlerts` (a volume at 50 % in minute 7 and 85 % in minute 8 of one quarter hour raises the warning at minute 8) was therefore green on the old tree too. A volume that crosses 80 % and 90 % within one 60 s report interval raises only the 90 % alert: that is the report cadence, not a defect, and the warning is for slower growth. **Samples.** A sample per minute (sequence = `floor(observed_at / 60 s)`, the unique key (database, source, producer, sequence) de-duplicates a second report of the minute) for every live database whose `observed_power` is not `hibernated`, and one per quarter hour for a hibernated one, which cannot grow: the one the first report of each quarter hour takes, in whichever minute it falls (the first version took only the report whose minute index is a multiple of 15, so one late or lost report at :00, :15, :30 or :45 left a quarter hour, and with four in a row an hour, without a sample: the agent stamps `observed_at` after its `lvs` inventory, which can wait on the LVM lock, and a failed volumes POST is not retried). Nothing reads the sequence as an order (the newest sample is chosen by `observed_at`; the sequence only breaks ties and names the key) and the old 15-minute sequences (about 2.0 million) cannot meet the minute indices (about 29.9 million); readers need no change: `health.disk`, `storage`, the rollup's maxima over the hour, the raw-sample prune (24 h after the hour ends) and the 10,000-samples-an-hour bound (an awake database writes about 121 an hour: 60 storage, 60 gateway, 1 backup). A report writes at most one statement per live database, the bound a first report of a quarter hour had before. The check for samples already taken reads at most the 15 rows of the quarter hour per database from the primary key (at most about 130 million index rows read a month for 200 awake databases, far inside the included 25 billion). **Row volume.** Samples a day: awake x 1,440 plus sleeping x 96. 300 databases, 5 % awake (15 and 285): 21,600 + 27,360 = 48,960 a day, 1,468,800 a month (28,800 and 864,000 before). 1,000 databases, 20 % awake (200 and 800): 288,000 + 76,800 = 364,800 a day, 10,944,000 a month (96,000 and 2,880,000 before). Kept rows (24 to 25 hours): about 50,000 and about 372,000. **D1 rows written:** the insert of one sample wrote 5 rows in the test D1 (the row, three indexes, the revision row the trigger upserts); the prune is bounded at the same 5, so 10 over a sample's life. 300 and 5 %: 7.3 million a month for inserts, 14.7 million with the prune (before 4.3 and 8.6): +6.0 million, 6 USD at 1 USD per million beyond the 50 million included a month. 1,000 and 20 %: 54.7 million and 109.4 million (before 14.4 and 28.8): +80.6 million, 81 USD (40 USD for the inserts alone); the included 50 million is shared with every other write of the account, so the increase is the marginal figure. Reads stay far inside the included 25 billion (about 1 billion a month for 200 awake databases, an estimate from the per-request hour count). Metrics only; the price is the integrator's. **Alert IDs.** `event_id` is now a function of the error: a version 8 UUID (RFC 9562; the first version said 5, but a name-based UUID from SHA-256 is no version 5, which is SHA-1, so an integrator with a UUIDv5 library would not have reproduced it) from the SHA-256 of a fixed domain string, the region, the kind and the sorted subjects the event lists (`alertEventId`; a kind without subjects, region and kind alone), instead of a random UUID per episode. `control_cron_step_failing` lists no database or node, so a failing cron step is its subject: the steps with 5 or more consecutive failures (`identity` of the observation, passed only to `alertEventId`; a step that failed once is not part of the error, and an active episode whose failing step changes keeps its event, as before), so `usage` failing one day and `health_alerts` the next have two IDs, and `usage` failing again has the first one again. The backup kinds list `d1` only (`infrastructureBackupHealth` expects one artifact kind), so region and kind are their whole identity there; `etcd` stays in the contract for old payloads. The same error keeps its ID through a clear and a recurrence; a different set of subjects is a new error with a new ID and is announced as before (only the ID generation changed: a subject that joins replaces the event, one that leaves changes nothing). The row (region, kind) reopens with the same ID (the `ON CONFLICT(region_id, kind)` takes precedence over the `UNIQUE(event_id)` that the same row also meets), `delivered_at` and `last_attempt_at` reset for the new episode, and the open episode is not sent again. `occurred_at` is the episode start, so a recurrence differs from the first episode in the payload. The listed subjects (at most 64) are hashed, because the payload and the re-announcement rule use that list and the full failing set is not read; a list cut at 64 changes ID when its head changes and returns to an earlier ID when the set returns; `unresolved` subjects are neither listed nor hashed. Consequences handled: the `Idempotency-Key` is `<event_id>:<occurred_at>` (Resend refuses a different body under a key it knows for 24 hours, so a recurrence under the bare `event_id` could not be mailed; not run against Resend), and the claim, the clearing and the replacement of an episode now compare the stored payload as well as the ID, because the ID no longer tells two episodes apart. Receivers deduplicate the pair `event_id` and `occurred_at`; a known `event_id` with a new `occurred_at` is a recurrence. The cron kinds are raised in every region and the region is part of the hash (`UNIQUE(event_id)` allows no ID in two rows), so one cron stall is announced as one event per region with a different ID each; one ID across regions would need a schema change and is not done. The `Idempotency-Key` code strict-parses the stored payload, like `infrastructureAlertStatus` does; a payload that does not parse is logged as `transport` and retried. **Deviation from the owner's request (owner decision).** Samples every minute are taken for databases that are not hibernated only; a hibernated database stays at one per quarter hour, so `health.disk` of a sleeping database reads `unknown` (no sample within 180 s) for 12 of every 15 minutes and `storage` is up to 15 minutes old. Sampling sleeping databases every minute too would add 285 x 1,344 x 30 = 11.5 million samples a month at 300 databases and 5 % awake (about 115 million D1 rows written with the prune, about 115 USD) and 800 x 1,344 x 30 = 32.3 million samples at 1,000 databases and 20 % awake (about 323 million rows, about 323 USD). **Review.** An independent review found six things, all checked against the code and all real: the hibernated mark (fixed failing-test-first), the version label (known answers recomputed with `node:crypto`), the shared ID of different cron steps (fixed failing-test-first; its backup half is not reachable, see above), and three texts (the stale OpenAPI sentence, the 15-minute wording of `health.disk`, and the replacement rule, which now names the exception that the three per-node warnings replace their episode only when none of the listed nodes still exceeds), and the receiver note (under Open). **Open (not done).** (a) Before the deploy: check or update the configured receiver so that it deduplicates the pair `event_id` and `occurred_at` (or the `Idempotency-Key`); a receiver on the old rule (deduplicate `event_id`) would acknowledge and then discard every recurrence (the receiver acknowledged four episodes on 2026-10-19 under the old contract). (b) Live acceptance after the deploy: one recurrence delivered through the real receiver with the same `event_id` and a new key; `storage` and `health.disk` of an awake database following a live fill minute by minute; and, separately, the 80 % and 90 % alerts for slow growth (the fill that passes both bands inside one report interval raises only the 90 % alert, the report cadence, not a defect). **Proof.** Red on the old tree: `node-intake.test.ts` 2 of 16 failed (per-minute and hibernated cadence; the newer sample behind `health.disk` and the rollup), `database-alerts.test.ts` 4 of 13, `infrastructure-alerts.test.ts` 3 of 12, `cron-watchdog.test.ts` 1 of 5, `infrastructure-backups.test.ts` 1 of 20, `alert-event-id.test.ts` (known-answer vectors) failed. Red on the first version of the branch, before the review fixes: the hibernated database that reports only in minutes 1 and 2 of a quarter hour got no sample (and a report at minute 7 of a quarter hour, none until minute 15), the test for a version 8 UUID failed, and two different failing cron steps got one `event_id`. Green after: all of them, the API suite (105 files, 987 tests) and the contracts tests (19 files, 224 tests); the known-answer IDs were recomputed independently with `node:crypto`, and `InfraAlert.shape.event_id` accepts version 8. The two payload-guard tests fail when the guards are removed. |
| Observations only when they change, with an anti-entropy, and the cron step `database_actors` in parallel, on branch `observation-delta` (2026-10-19) | **Found live (EU1, release `sha-bb85de7d1bb0`, 293 databases, about 280 asleep).** Every 60 s full pull of the desired page made the agent queue and post an observation of every database (`accept` calls `set`, which queued the observation), and the API applied each with a D1 compare-and-set and an Actor publish (`nodeObservations`, 8 at a time, 12 s budget). One report of 64 databases took 4.3–5.4 s (slowest single database 1.1–1.2 s; `observation_commit` `d1_ms` about 70 ms, `publish_ms` 100–470 ms), so a pass over 293 databases kept the API and the NodeActor busy for about 25 s of every 60 s with nothing changing (an earlier measurement: 200 observations in 10.2 s). The load grows with the number of databases (about 700 fill the minute; the placement rule has no limit), a create finished after 110–130 s although the node needs about 60 s (its `ready` report waited behind the pass), and the NodeActor link flapped. **Agent (`agent/report.rs`).** `Reports` holds, per database, the stored form of the observation the API last accepted and when (accepted is the API's 200 answer with the verdict `accept` for that database: the new contract `NodeObservationAnswer`, which the route already answered, is read by `Link::post_observation`, which now returns the accepted identifiers). The stored form is what `applyObservation` and `archiveHealth` keep: the archive's last segment and backlog are not in it, only `failing_since` and whether the files are known. `take_report` drops what equals it, so a pull of an unchanged page queues nothing; every change (state, generation, operation, power, instance, proof, backup, archive failure, refusal, roles) differs and goes at once. A report that failed, timed out or lost its answer holds none of its databases (the API may have applied it), `stale` and `conflict` leave the database unaccepted and the next pull queues it again, and a restarted agent holds nothing, so its first pull says every database once through the existing batches of 64 (one report after the other); the API applies each of them as a report of an unchanged database, a publish each, so that pull costs at most what one pass did before (about 25 s of API time at 293 databases), once for each start of the agent. **Anti-entropy.** `NODE_OBSERVATION_RESEND_MS`, 15 minutes, is a contract constant that the agent reads from the generated JSON: at 300 databases that is 20 observations a minute where a pass was 300, and the period is never shortened. Each database goes again at the turn its identifier fixes (the first 8 bytes of the SHA-256 of the identifier modulo the period, so the databases never come in one minute whenever the agent started), checked each minute (`Intervals.resend`), at most one report (64) a minute, the one accepted longest ago first; at 300 databases the busiest of 30 minutes held 40 or fewer (test). A database Cloudflare waits for (an entry with an origin, which is a build, a restore or a move target, or an open move order) is not deduplicated: the API's create, restore and move steps wait for the next report that names it (`progressMoves` reads the report), so every pull says it as before. **API.** `applyObservation` reads the row once (as before) and, when a `ready` or `asleep` observation equals what D1 holds in every column the update writes (state, power, generation, the runtime but for its time, the proof, message, archive health and time, backup health and completion), and a suspended database's sleep has the event of its generation written, writes nothing but `backup_observed_at` (forward only, and not `updated_at`) and publishes the Actor's admission snapshot as before, with the same 1 s answer bound: a publish that fails within it fails the report, which the node sends again, and the sent-again report of a state D1 already holds is the repair of the snapshot (a report of an unchanged database repaired it every minute before; now it does once in each period, one publish per resend, about 20 a minute at 300 databases). An observation that differs takes the whole path, and so does the asleep report of a suspended database whose sleep has no event of that generation yet: a cold resize of a sleeping database takes the next generation and observes it at once, so the node's asleep report of that generation equals the row, yet no report has run the statements that end the resize and write the sleep's event, which meters the new resources. An independent review found it twice. First the resize stayed `pending` until `operation_timeout` failed it after 20 minutes. The first fix looked for an open operation, but a resize that `operation_timeout` failed while the node, its agent or its link was down is no longer open, and the report that came later was taken as held, so the event of the generation was never written (the old tree writes it when the report arrives). The row now holds the sleep only once that event exists, which only a report writes, and a database that cannot show it takes the whole path as before. A role removal needs no such clause: only a report that names roles ends one, a sleep ends the ones that wait before it, and a removal asked of a suspended database is created ended. The report's own handling of its databases uses the shared `eachBounded`, which `reconcileDatabaseActors` (the cron step `database_actors`) now uses too: 8 publishes at a time (`ACTOR_RECONCILE_CONCURRENCY`), no new start after 20 s (`ACTOR_RECONCILE_BUDGET_MS`), and the cursor stands after the last database published (a page of 200 took 50–220 s, derived, one after the other; not measured). **Readers of a database's observation time** (the comment at `NODE_OBSERVATION_RESEND_MS` has the list): only `backup_observed_at` needs the period, so `health.backup` (database and operator listing) and the alert `database_base_backup_stale` judge a report fresh for `NODE_DATABASE_STALE_MS` (45 minutes, three periods) where they judged 180 s, which would have made every unchanged database `unknown` and silenced the alert; `regional_node_stale`, placement, `thin_pool_unobserved`, the node alerts and the fleet release state read the node's own sample (60 s) and stay at 180 s; storage samples, `health.disk` and the volume alerts come from the volume report (60 s); the recovery points and the proof are written when they change and nothing judges their age; `database_actors` reads D1 rows. The Actor's admission snapshot is published by every report that names the database, changed or not, so the period is also how often a snapshot that a lost publish left behind is repaired; the cron step `database_actors` (up to 200 databases a minute, 8 at a time) is the other repair. A report that does not name a database says nothing of it: there is no "missing means gone" in `nodeObservations`, and a test names two databases, reports one four times and finds the other's row identical. **Tests, red first.** `node-observation-delta.test.ts` (8; measured against the old tree, the API sources of `bb85de7d`: 4 red, for the reasons the change is about: `updated_at` moved on an unchanged observation, ready and asleep, and on a backlog-only change, and `health.backup` became `unknown` for a report ten minutes old. The publish count no longer tells the trees apart, because the old tree also published once per report. The other 4 pass on the old tree and guard what the delta must not break: the report sent again after a failed publish, the two cold-resize tests (the asleep report of the new generation ends the resize; and writes the sleep's event after the resize was failed by `operation_timeout`) and the test that a report which does not name a database says nothing of it. Against the first version of the branch (`c4027213`) 6 of the 8 are red: an unchanged observation published nothing there, so the three tests that count its publish, the repair after a failed publish and both cold-resize tests failed. The previous commit (`159db25f`) was red on the timed-out resize only. An unchanged observation now publishes once, and the three tests that counted no publish count one), `actor-reconcile-concurrency.test.ts` (3, all red: 1 publish at a time, a 250 ms budget still published all 60), the agent's `an_observation_the_api_accepted_is_posted_again_only_when_it_changes_and_one_it_did_not_accept_is_posted_again` (red: `[2, 2, 2]` posts of three databases where `[1, 2, 1]` is right, one of them answered `conflict`); also in `report.rs` the turn and stagger tests, the cap, the lost answer, and the pull, move-hold and restart test on a real PostgreSQL 18; `database-alerts.test.ts` and `operational-health.test.ts` follow the new window, and the contract has six vectors of `NodeObservationAnswer`. **Merged with main (2026-10-20: the resume race and the host files).** The order results of the resume race (`failedWake`, `recoverFailedResume`) and the delta stand side by side. The API takes a report's orders after its databases, whether or not it already held them. An order result is never held: it leaves with the report that carries it, and `unsent` puts it back after a failed or lost answer, also when every database of that report is held (a test). A failed resume suspends the database at a generation above the one the node reported, so the node's next asleep report differs from the row and takes the whole path, and `holds` never takes it for held. The hello's `os.host_files_sha256` is the node's own record, which no observation reads. One interplay was found by `actor-admission-imports.test.ts` and fixed: `database-actor-sync.ts` imported `bounded.ts` statically, which made a ticket claim evaluate a ninth module before its answer where the test pins eight; `reconcileDatabaseActors` now imports it when the cron reads a page. **Deploy order and live acceptance:** 11.2, row "5 Acceptance: steady-state observation load" (it holds the deploy order too). |
| A failed or timed-out resume suspends only a sleeping database, and a resume order that meets a stale page waits for it, on branch `resume-race` (2026-10-19) | **Found live** (EU1, release `sha-a9bcb8195bab`, 1,000-cycle acceptance): three of 24 databases failed `database.resume` with `suspended` ("wake outcome=refused reason=suspended total_ms=1") and stayed desired running, observed hibernated, with a failed operation, for nothing to wake: a connection wakes only an idle sleeper, the node runs an order once and the API ended the resume. **Cause.** The node's `try_wake` refused with `suspended` from the entry's desired state before it compared generations, so the order of generation N+1 against an entry still at (N, Suspended) was final. The node gets that pair from a pull that carries the order and not its entry. Every pull carries every open order of the node whatever it asks for (`pageOrders` runs for every query) and the entries only of what it names (`query.ids`, the `?after` page). First, a pull of named databases (the hint that every suspend, resume and wake makes) for database A that reads the orders after the resume of database B committed hands the node B's order with B's old entry (checked: `?ids=A` returned A and B's wake order, not B's entry); no node with more than 200 databases is needed. Second, a full pull across pages of 200 entries does the same between an early page and a later one. **Node.** A request above the entry of a suspended database is now `desired_stale` (stale `generation`); `suspended` stays for a request of the entry's generation or an older one, which is all a connection ticket can be, because only an idle sleeper is wakeable and the Actor issues no ticket for a manual suspension. `obey_wake` waits for the page (`page_reaches`, the backoff of the recovery) when it is refused `desired_stale` while the entry is below the order's generation, and asks again from the entry; no start happens from an entry that is behind, a request that the page has passed ends at once with the entry's own refusal, and a wait that does not end (an entry that does not come) costs one local ask every 30 s at most and ends with the resume's 20-minute timeout. **API.** `recoverFailedResume` ends a resume the node could not carry out with the node's reason and suspends the database again at the next generation as it was before the resume (manual, or idle by a hibernation), by an operation the node's report of the sleep ends like any suspension; a node that starts the database late finds the newer generation suspended and sleeps it again. The revert is gated on D1 knowing the database asleep (`observed_power` hibernated): the resume that a refused sleep makes (`recoverQuiescence`) is of a database that runs, is not stuck (its ready report admits it), and a suspension would have stopped what the owner wants running and flapped with the next refused sleep, so there only the operation fails (found by the review, red first). A result whose recovery lost three compare-and-sets throws a 409 (`generation_changed`) instead of returning 0, so the node, which sends a result once and posts a rejected report again, sends it again (not a 5xx, so the batch does not shrink). The cron's power timeout takes a database that is to run and sleeps with a resume older than 20 minutes through the same revert (`operation_timeout`), in pages of 8 with the suspension timeouts' cursor, and the generic operation sweep leaves those resumes to it, so a resume waiting for its turn is not failed first (red first: without the exclusion the ninth of nine is failed without a revert). The `Changed` bullet in 3.4 says that a failed resume suspends a database that sleeps again and a database that runs keeps running. No contract change; either deploy order is wire-compatible (an old agent's stale `suspended` refusal of a sleeping database now reverts it, and of a running one only fails the operation). **Tests, failing first.** Node (real PostgreSQL 18): the order that comes before its entry waits, says nothing to Cloudflare while it waits and ends done once the page has caught up, and the connection after it is the same start released at once; an order the page has passed ends at once and is not waited for; the wake refusal by generation. A twenty-cycle repeat of the same scenario was removed on review (12 s of a 60 s suite, one deterministic case). API: the failed resume of a sleeping database (manual and idle) is suspended again and the node's confirmation of the sleep ends it; the late, other node's and ended results change nothing; the resume a refused sleep made only fails; the lost compare-and-sets answer 409 and the same result posted again ends the resume; nine timed-out resumes are reverted eight and one per run; a timed-out resume of a database that runs only fails. Gates: contracts (generators, 224 tests), typecheck, eslint, prettier, API 993 tests, node agent fmt, clippy `-D warnings` and 520 lib tests (13 ignored) in the pinned builder image with PostgreSQL 18, contrib, netcat, perl and procps as a non-root user, and Mermaider. Independent review by Opus: changes (5 required, fixed; 2 optional notes rejected with evidence). **Open:** the acceptance cycle (suspend, resume, query, repeated) on EU1 after the release that carries it, with the three failures as the reference. **Round 2 (`a073809d`):** an unproven suspension of a database that D1 knows asleep is no longer undone into a resume. After 20 minutes the generic operation sweep fails it with operation_timeout and the database stays suspended. Before this, the revert's suspend and the resume made an endless cycle every 20 minutes while no node reported: two generations, two operation rows and two admission mutations per cycle, and a returning node started a database whose owner's last request was a suspend. The power-timeout SELECT and the sweep exclusion share one predicate (undoneByPowerTimeout in cron.ts), so each timed-out power operation has exactly one owner. An awake database whose suspension the node never proves still goes back to running (power_timeout). Proof: a cron test with a sleeping, resumed database and no node reports, aged 21 minutes before each of 5 rounds, shows one revert (g3 to g4) and then no change in state, operation rows or admission epoch; it was red before (g5 running, a resume again) and is green now. Full API suite 995 of 995, typecheck, eslint, prettier and Mermaider green. Still open, named honestly: (a) the stuck state "desired running, observed hibernated, resume failed" remains for a node's `error` observation of a resume or wake (applyObservation fails the operation and calls no revert; the database stays desired running with observed state error until a new request or the node's own retry, which was not examined here); (b) a failed suspension that a late node report proves stays failed (succeed touches only pending or running), so a resize or profile rollout of the sleeping database does not count it as confirmed cold until it is resumed and suspended again, while an idle sleeper stays wakeable by connection; (c) each owner resume of a sleeper whose node stays silent costs one revert after 20 minutes (bounded by the owner's requests, no longer by the clock); (d) the node still keeps one waiting obey_wake task per resume whose entry never arrives (apply.rs), now bounded by the owner's resumes. Not pushed, not merged. One narrow edge the verifier named stays open until it is seen live: a refusal (`power_busy` or `power_archive`) that reaches the API after the sweep has already failed that generation's suspension is answered `conflict` for good, so a database that started late after a partition of more than 40 minutes stays running with nothing pending until the owner resumes and suspends it again; data is not affected. Deploy order: the API first, then the agents. |
| EU1 reinstalled from the volume-sandbox image (2026-10-19, owner's go) | The 301 test databases were deleted through the API first, so the reinstall guard had nothing to block. `mark-lost` of `nod_s0rjgbhte0utjauv53hf` at 18:40:33 UTC, fenced (Tunnel deleted, instance off) by 18:41:03; release `sha-bb85de7d1bb0` (main `bb85de7d`, signed) assigned; AddNode recover (`op_05iup9geqwa7vy1di71t`) reached `ready` after 231 s as `nod_d4wzucremvt1xgev52a0`, converged on the release, with all of the ports 22, 443, 5432 and 50000 timing out from outside. PID 1's mountinfo holds 27 lines and one `pgcf` entry with the agent's volumes mounted, so no volume is in PID 1's namespace. Recover restores nothing: databases are recreated (a restore is a new database). First create batch on the fresh node took about 3.5 min, later ones 110 to 130 s with eight in flight (the steady-state observation load, 11.2). |

EU1 wrote the key (`shell_ca_written`, 19:49:23 UTC) and `node-shell.ts` ran commands on it as root through Cloudflare. The operator's machine needed `cloudflared` (Homebrew, 2026.10.1, installed with the owner's yes). The organization has no one-time-PIN login, so only the service token path works. |
| Host files ship with the signed release: units and configuration change without a new image, on branch `host-files` (2026-10-19) | **Why.** The owner asked how a change like V4h's units reaches a node without a new image; until now only the binaries and the package lock did, and the image's files reached a node only through a reinstall. **Contracts.** A release carries `os.host_files` (`HostFiles`: one to 64 entries ascending by path, each `{path, kind: file or symlink-to-dev-null, mode: 0644 or 0755, sha256, size_bytes (at most 1 MiB), apply: daemon_reload, next_start, sysusers, tmpfiles or restart_unit:<unit>}`; a path is directly in one of 12 allow-listed directories, and `restart_unit` names only the agent, gateway, cloudflared, ssh, journald or systemd-sysctl service) and `os.host_files_sha256`, which a node that holds them reports; the signed manifest is `pgcf-release-manifest/v2` with one `os.host_file=` line each (the spec is version 3, and nothing signed as v1 is in the field); `RELEASE_REFUSALS` gains `host_file_path_refused`, `host_file_mode_refused`, `host_file_apply_refused` and `host_files_invalid`; the hello's `NodeOs` carries `host_files_sha256`. **The node's page states the shape of the list and no rule** (`NodeHostFiles`: text for the path, the mode and the way to take effect, a number for the size, at most 1024 entries, and the two kinds, whose facts lines are a format): a release that widens a rule (a seventh unit, a thirteenth directory, another way to take effect) is read by an agent of an older build, which installs its binaries and refuses its files, and never freezes a node. The shared vector holds a list of seven files with every kind, mode and way to take effect with its manifest text and digest, and the agent reads the directories, modes, units, applies, bounds and directory name from the generated constants. **One definition.** `infra/node-image/host-files.ts` derives the list from `mkosi.extra`: a file in an allow-listed directory is a host file with a row in `HOST_FILE_APPLY`, every other file is `IMAGE_ONLY` with its reason (bootloader, `fstab`, firewall, sshd's data files, the generator mask, presets, partitioning, the links to the binaries), and the build and the registration stop at a file that neither table names or a row that names no file; `pgcf-node.json` pins the list's digest and the registration refuses a checkout whose list differs; the builder gives the tree the modes the list states (`builder/normalize-modes.sh`: 0755 for what the owner executes, else 0644, 0755 for a directory), so a checkout made under umask 002 does not make every node rewrite all its host files at its first start. **API.** Migration `0079` (`nodes.os_host_files_sha256`; removes the releases registered without host files and their region assignments, as 0072 and 0077 did, the nodes keep what they run); the registration states the digest and the API refuses one that is not its list's (400) and every host file object that is not in the bucket at its size with the sha256 R2 verified (`node-images/<id>/host-files/<sha256>`); `GET /node/v1/releases/<id>/host-files/<sha256>` serves only a digest the release's own list holds, so no path is ever in a URL; the page carries the list and not the digest; `os_converged` compares kernel, packages and host files, and `GET /v1/nodes/{id}/release` keeps speaking of the binaries (the maintenance cycle needs it `converged` first); the signer's body bound is 32 KiB for the longest list. **Agent** (`hostfiles.rs`, `service.rs`, 9.3): `update::verify` checks the signature only, and the agent's own rules are held by the agent that writes (`Service::host_files`), after the binaries, once per release, as `release_refused` for the files only; atomic writes (hidden file, `fsync`, rename, `fsync` of the directory) below `/`, no link followed in the chain or at the target, no directory made but the last allow-listed one; the record of owned paths and of what is left for systemd (the boot, the reload, the runs, the restarts) saved before the first write; one reload, then `systemd-sysusers <file>` and `systemd-tmpfiles --create <file>` on the files that say so (the restarts wait for them), then the restarts in name order, each tried, the failures kept and tried again after a wait (5 s doubling to 5 min), the agent's own last; a reload and restarts of an earlier boot dropped with the boot id; a dropped file restarts the unit it was applied with, and a path an earlier list named is removed whoever wrote it; the agent's own unit written to `/run/systemd/system` and moved to its place by the agent that runs it after its first page (`promote`: not while its own restart is owed, never in a process about to leave); the digest read from the disk, which is the release's only when nothing is left for systemd. **The order** is one rule: the binaries first, the files after, written by the agent that runs the release's own binary; an agent whose binary was replaced writes nothing more, even from a later page. Two rules for the author of a change are stated in 9.3 and the node image README and checked by nobody: a new agent starts under the previous agent unit, and a host file works with the packages of the release before. `Machine` gains `daemon_reload`, `run_sysusers` and `run_tmpfiles`; the test machine's systemd follows the real one (its search path `/etc`, `/run`, `/usr/lib`, the definition of the last reload at a restart, a boot that empties `/run`, tools that fail on a missing or malformed file). **Review (Opus, nine findings; each checked against the code).** (1) Fixed: the page was strict, so a release that widened a rule would have made every older agent refuse the whole page; now shape only, and the rules at the write (a test: a signed list with a unit the agent has no name for installs the binaries, is refused once with `host_file_apply_refused`, writes nothing and tells systemd nothing, and the next release with an allowed list is written). (2) Fixed: measured on Debian 13 (systemd 257.13), `systemd-sysusers.service` has `ConditionNeedsUpdate=|/etc`, which a changed file in `/usr/lib/sysusers.d` does not satisfy, so the `reboot` class never made a user; it is replaced by the runs of the two tools, which are idempotent (run twice on the image's files: users and directories made once, exit 0 both times; a missing or malformed file exits 1), and the maintenance cycle's reboot path is deleted. (3) Fixed: the agent's own unit goes to `/run` first (the lab test measures that a runtime unit wins over the image's, that removing it and reloading restores the image's, and that the promotion reads the new file), the two ordering rules are stated, and recovery has an entry. (4) Fixed: the digest is another while a reload, run or restart of the current boot is in the record, and a dropped file restarts its unit. (5) Fixed: each restart is tried, and a failure waits. (6a) Partly rejected: after a `daemon-reload` a running instance's properties are the file's and its next stop uses the new `ExecStop=`, `KillSignal=` and `TimeoutStopSec=` (the reviewer is right), but the kernel's `memory.max`, `pids.max` and `cpu.weight` of its cgroup were unchanged two seconds after the reload and changed only at the restart (measured, systemd 257.13, privileged container, cgroup v2), so resource limits are not re-applied; the documents say what was measured. (6b, 6c) Fixed in the documents and a test name. (7) Fixed: two lab tests against a real systemd (the D-Bus `Reload` and the `RestartUnit` after it on a running template instance; the runtime unit), the stringly test removed, and a test for each of 1, 4 and 5. (8) Fixed by the builder. (9) Rejected: the install restarts the gateway under the old unit, then the host files restart it; skipping the first would tie the new binary's start to the success of the files, which return before any restart on a refused list, a failed fetch or a failed apply, and would leave the old gateway running over a replaced binary for the cost of one more reconnect, a rare coincidence of a gateway binary and a gateway unit in one release. **Tests.** Contracts 20 files and 237 tests (`host-files.test.ts`: the allow-list with more than twenty refused paths, modes, applies, order and bound, the manifest text and what it covers, the digest, the page that states the shape and no rule; the migration; the vector); API 104 files and 982 tests; signer 35; node image tooling 51 (`host-files.test.ts` 7: the builder's modes; `units.test.ts`: every unit takes effect the way it needs, users and directories through their tools); CI and operations scripts 107; node agent 553 passed and 15 ignored (13 kernel tests and the 2 lab tests), in a container built from the pinned builder image (Alpine, PostgreSQL 18 and contrib, non-root; `hostfiles` also as root): `hostfiles` 22, `update` (the signature says nothing of the agent's rules), `service` 11 for host files (the order with the agent that leaves and the agent that promotes its unit, files at once and a second page that changes nothing, a tampered list refused with nothing fetched or written, a list whose rules the agent lacks, a failed reload, a restart not repeated by every page, a unit that cannot restart keeping neither the others nor the agent from theirs, a restart that failed never claimed after an agent restart or reboot, a failed run holding the restarts, users and directories made by their tools without a reboot, an agent that has not left for its unit not promoting it). The fixes of the agent were checked by reverting each one (nine reverts: each turns its tests red); the contract page test, the builder test and the apply vocabulary were red before their code. The two lab tests ran four times in a Debian 13 container with systemd 257.13 as PID 1 (privileged, cgroup v2). Format, lint, typecheck, clippy with `-D warnings`, Mermaider. **Not covered, on purpose.** (1) Drift at run time: the disk is read at the agent's start and after each apply, not on every page (a file changed by hand is found at the next start). (2) No migration hook, and nothing refuses a release that changes `MountImages=` or `PrivateMounts=` for a region with running nodes: it is written to every node, and the documents say it must not be assigned (V4h reaches EU1 with the reinstall already decided). (3) An agent of an earlier build cannot read the page of a release with host files and its hello is refused: EU1 runs the agent of main `bb85de7d` (reinstalled before this change), so it gets the first agent that writes host files by a one-time replacement of the agent binary through the break-glass shell, which the Open row of 11.2 and `operator-installation.md` ("Replacing the agent by hand") give step by step; no code supports it (Dev: newest only). (4) Nothing ran on EU1: the writes as root on Debian's own `/etc` and `/usr/lib`, `restart` of ssh, journald and systemd-sysctl, the Contabo restart of a node whose new agent unit cannot start, and the tools through the agent's `Node` machine were tested against the test machine, a temporary directory, the lab container and the tools by hand only. (5) The allow-list also names the `/usr/lib` form of sysctl.d, sysusers.d, tmpfiles.d and journald.conf.d, because the image keeps its files there and not in `/etc`. (6) A node whose new agent unit cannot start runs the image's unit after a restart, and then writes the same unit again (the release has to be replaced first): `recovery.md` says so. **After the merge with main (2026-10-19)**: the migration `0079` does not collide (main ends at `0078`); the OpenAPI text of `os_converged` no longer names a reboot (the class is gone) and `KillMode=` is no longer listed among the measured stop settings (the lab measured `KillSignal=`, `TimeoutStopSec=` and `ExecStop=`); and the verifier's hardening is made, with its failing test first: the runs of `systemd-sysusers` and `systemd-tmpfiles` come before the reload (they read the files from disk, which are all written by then), so a `Restart=always` unit that crashes after the reload comes back under its new definition with its user and directories in place, and a failed run holds the reload and the restarts back |

With an administrator key and write access to the archive bucket, someone can register a release with an image of their own and any signature, assign it, and add or recover a node: it boots that image, whose agent trusts the attacker's key. A trust anchor outside the API exists, which an earlier version of this row denied: the installer script is Worker code (`node-installer-files.generated.ts`, generated from `infra/node-image/installer/install.sh` and bundled in the Worker), which an administrator key cannot change. The Worker can carry the release public key to the installer, and CI can sign the image's digests (the compressed and the raw sha256 and size, which the spec already holds), so that the installer verifies before it writes; what that needs is a signature of the image digests in the manifest (or a second manifest) and a verifier in the rescue system, whose tools have to be checked. The owner decides: build it as the follow-up, or accept the risk (the agent baked into an image does not help here, because the agent of an attacker's image verifies against the attacker's key). What stays outside either way: whoever can deploy the Worker, which is the Cloudflare account, and a rollback to an older signed release, which an administrator can still assign |

(2) A release that changes a comment of `pgcf-agent.service`: the agent writes it to `/run/systemd/system/pgcf-agent.service`, reloads, leaves, and the agent that starts moves it after its first page (`agent_unit_promoted`); `systemctl show pgcf-agent -p FragmentPath` names `/usr/lib/systemd/system`, the copy in `/run` is gone, and `os_converged` is false until then and true after. | Sandbox v4h live acceptance on EU1 (gating) | Code, tests and labs are done (11.1); not deployed, and not measured on EU1 | **Rollout order.** The units, the masks and the device-policy checks of this change ship with the image: the mount topology cannot be moved by files, and the first agent that can write host files (9.3, 11.1) comes with an image too, so for this change a release still changes only binaries. A new agent on the current image works (it makes the links, which the old units ignore) and gains nothing: the volumes stay in PID 1's namespace and a start still grows with them. An agent binary from before this change on a V4h image cannot start any database (every start fails its `AssertPathExists=`, because that agent makes no link), so there is no rollback to an older release after the image, and a region with a node on the V4h image is never assigned an older release; nothing enforces this. So the release whose agent makes the link goes first, and the image after it. **How EU1 gets the image:** a reinstall by AddNode in recover mode on the existing EU1 instance (`POST /v1/nodes/additions` with `mode` `recover`, the predecessor node and its `node_uid`), the route of the Stage 4 install (196.5 s from the rescue request to an enrolled node, with no databases); no update path writes the image's files to a running node. **What it costs:** the reinstall overwrites the disk, so every database on EU1 is gone from the node and is rebuilt from its archive in R2 (a restore from its base backup and WAL; recovery.md: a new database with a new ID that the integrator rebinds), and its databases are out of service until each restore has finished (about 300 of them; restore time at that count is not measured). The reinstall guard refuses recover mode while any database's current generation lacks a proven-sleep record, and the owner's override (`POST /v1/nodes/{id}/reinstall-override`) accepts the loss of unarchived WAL. Because the new agent makes every volume again, each has the 4 MiB gap (11.1); without the reinstall the databases stay as they are (recreate them or shrink offline). It needs the owner's go. **Measure on EU1, gating the rollout to any other node and the go-live gate:** (a) agent restart to the first served wake, and the first request after an agent restart, at EU1's number of volumes (301): the agent serves no wake until every volume is mounted again (lab: 0.17 / 0.30 / 0.89 s for 50 / 100 / 300 volumes, about 3 ms each, serial, under the storage lock, so a wake that arrives during `mount_all` waits for it), and the mounts of sleeping databases end with the agent while a running unit keeps its own, so their page cache is gone (the plan measured about 300 ms more on a cold first request). If the numbers are too high the remedy is to keep the agent's namespace across restarts (bind it under `/run` and enter it before the runtime starts) or to serve wakes while the mounts are made; neither is built. (b) The start of `pgcf-pg@` against the 105 ms at 17 and 245 ms at 164 volumes before the change (the effect is measured in the lab only, and the live/lab factor is unverified, 11.1). (c) The cost of `MountImages=` dissection per start, and of the `daemon-reload` scan of one drop-in directory per database. (d) One real barman base backup, WAL archive, WAL fetch and restore as `systemd-run` jobs with `MountImages=`. (e) The boot-test checks of `guest.sh`. **What did not run:** the `guest.sh` checks (they run only on KVM CI and on EU1; the block-device function was validated in the lab against a transient sandboxed unit), anything on EU1, a real barman backup, WAL archive, WAL fetch or restore through `MountImages=` (the job lab ran `touch` and `ls`), and a wake that arrives during `mount_all` at the service level (the agent tests drive the service with the double, the lab drives `mount_all` alone). The storage, units, node and job labs are privileged and do not run in CI. **What still scales with N:** the burst of mounts at the agent's start; the agent's wake steps that read the volume list (an `lvs` of 150 to 186 ms at 1,000 volumes whenever the list is older than 20 s); cgroup realize for the units of `pgcf.slice` and the per-unit drop-in directories that `daemon-reload` reads (neither is measured on a node, and the lab found no growth of a start with up to 900 running units); and `lvs` itself. **Known and unchanged:** a refused unmount holds the storage lock for 250 ms and a refused `lvremove` for about 5 s; `delete()` ignores `Busy`, so a job that still holds the volume leaves its logical volume in place after the database is reported deleted (the propagated EBUSY of the old code did the same; a superseded order reports `volume_not_removed` instead). |

Make at least 20 cold wakes of databases that slept at least a few minutes (from a Worker through the public hostname, as in 5.3; 20 more after 60 min or more of sleep if the cold page cache is to be told apart). State in that row whether the CPU quota throttled the login (`nr_throttled` above 0 within the first 2 s, and the `throttled_usec` of those wakes), and whether the slow wakes are the ones with steal. then `SHOW` of the nine settings on a small database, the child processes of its idle postmaster (the logical replication launcher no longer runs), the `start` phase of `phases_ms` over at least 30 cold wakes before and after the release (p50 and p95), and the 5.1 PITR of a database that was backed up before the release into a small class, which needs the recovery settings of 7.8; (2) `noatime`: `/proc/self/mountinfo` shows it on a volume mounted by the release (a new database at once, an older one after the next reboot, as a mounted volume keeps its options), and the write bytes of the volume's device in `/proc/diskstats` over 5 minutes of the same read-only workload on a `noatime` and on a `relatime` volume; (3) `--noflush`: the write counters (writes completed and sectors written) of the pool's metadata device, the `pgcf-pgcf_thinpool_tmeta` device-mapper device with its `dm-N` line in `/proc/diskstats`, over an idle hour before and after the release, and optionally over a steady small write load on one awake database (not the pool's transaction ID, which only LVM changes), and one status call with and one without the flag, taken back to back, whose block counts must agree;
